1.1 Propositions and logical operations
Learn to:

    Determine whether a statement is a proposition.
    Express English propositions using logical notation.
    Construct truth tables for propositions involving conjunction, disjunction, inclusive or, exclusive or, and negation.
    Evaluate compound propositions using the truth values of the individual propositions.

Logic is the study of formal reasoning. A statement in a spoken language, such as in English, is often ambiguous in its meaning. By contrast, a statement in logic always has a well-defined meaning. Logic is important in mathematics for proving theorems. Logic is also used in computer science in areas such as artificial intelligence for automated reasoning and in designing digital circuits. Logic is useful in any field in which it is important to make precise statements. In law, logic defines the implications of a particular law. In medicine, logic precisely specifies the conditions under which a particular diagnosis applies.

The most basic element in logic is a proposition. A proposition is a statement that is either true or false.
Table 1.1.1: Examples of propositions: Statements that are either true or false.
Proposition	Truth value
There are an infinite number of prime numbers. 	True
is an even number. 	False
Feedback?

Propositions are typically declarative sentences. For example, the following are not propositions.
Table 1.1.2: English sentences that are not propositions.
Sentence	Comment
What time is it? 	A question, not a proposition. A question is neither true nor false.
Are you awake? 	Even a yes/no question is neither true nor false, so is not a proposition.
Have a nice day. 	A command, not a proposition. A command is neither true nor false.
Feedback?

A proposition's truth value is a value indicating whether the proposition is true or false. A proposition is still a proposition whether its truth value is known to be true, known to be false, unknown, or a matter of opinion. The following are all propositions.
Table 1.1.3: Examples of propositions and their truth values.
Proposition	Comment
Two plus two is four. 	Truth value is true.
Two plus two is five. 	Truth value is false.
Monday will be cloudy. 	Truth value is unknown.
The movie was funny. 	Truth value is a matter of opinion.
Feedback?

The conjunction operation

Propositional variables such as , , and are used to denote arbitrary propositions, as in:

January has days.
February has days.

A compound proposition is created by connecting individual propositions with logical operations. A logical operation combines propositions using a particular composition rule. For example, the conjunction operation is denoted by The proposition is read " and " and is called the conjunction of and . is true if both is true and is true. is false if is false, is false, or both are false.

Using the definitions for given above, the proposition is expressed in English as:

January has days and February has days.

Proposition 's truth value is true: January does have days. Proposition 's truth value is false: February does not have days. The compound proposition is therefore false, because it is not the case that both propositions are true.

A truth table shows the truth value of a compound proposition for every possible combination of truth values for the variables in the compound proposition. Every row in the truth table shows a particular truth value for each variable, along with the compound proposition's corresponding truth value. Below is the truth table for , where represents true and represents false.

Static figure: The following table is shown:
\(p\) 	\(q\) 	\(p \land q\) 	Explanation
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(p \land q\) is true only when both \(p\) and \(q\) are true.
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(p \land q\) is false for all other combinations.
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\)
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\)
Step 1: \(p \land q\) is true only when both \(p\) and \(q\) are true. Step 2: \(p \land q\) is false for all other combinations.

Different ways to express a conjunction in English

Define the propositional variables and as:

The sauce looks disgusting.
The sauce tastes delicious.

There are many ways to express the proposition in English. The sentences below have slightly different meanings in English but correspond to the same logical meaning.
Table 1.1.4: Examples of different ways to express a conjunction in English.
and 	The sauce looks disgusting and tastes delicious.
, but 	The sauce looks disgusting, but tastes delicious.
Despite the fact that , 	Despite the fact that the sauce looks disgusting, it tastes delicious.
Although , 	Although the sauce looks disgusting, it tastes delicious.
Feedback?
The disjunction operation

The disjunction operation is denoted by The proposition is read " or " and is called the disjunction of and is true if either or is true, or if both are true. The proposition is false only if both and are false. Using the same and from the example above, is the statement:

January has days or February has days.

The proposition is true because January does have days. The truth table for the operation is given below.
participation activity
1.1.3: Truth table for the disjunction operation.
Start
Static figure: The following table is shown:
\(p\) 	\(q\) 	\(p \lor q\) 	Explanation
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(p \lor q\) is true when either \(p\) or \(q\) is true.
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\)
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\)
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(p \lor q\) is false only when \(p\) and \(q\) are both false.
Step 1: \(p \lor q\) is true when either \(p\) or \(q\) is true. Step 2: \(p \lor q\) is false only when \(p\) and \(q\) are both false.
Captions
Feedback?
Ambiguity of "or" in English

The meaning of the word "or" in English depends on context. Often when the word "or" is used in English, the intended meaning is that one or the other of two things is true, but not both. One would normally understand the sentence "Lucy is going to the park or the movie" to mean that Lucy is either going to the park, or is going to the movie, but not both. Such an either/or meaning corresponds to the "exclusive or" operation in logic. The exclusive or of and evaluates to true when is true and is false or when is true and is false. The inclusive or operation is the same as the disjunction () operation and evaluates to true when one or both of the propositions are true. For example, "Lucy opens the windows or doors when warm" means she opens windows, doors, or possibly both. Since the inclusive or is most common in logic, it is just called "or" for short.
participation activity

The negation operation acts on just one proposition and has the effect of reversing the truth value of the proposition. The negation of proposition is denoted and is read as "not ." Since the negation operation only acts on a single proposition, its truth table has only two rows for the proposition's two possible truth values.
participation activity

Start
Static figure: The following table is shown:
\(p\) 	\(\neg p\) 	Explanation
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\neg p\) is simply the opposite of \(p\).
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\)
Step 1: The truth value of \(\neg p\) is the opposite of the truth value of \(p\). \(\neg \text{T}\) is \(\text{F}\) and \(\neg\text{F}\) is \(\text{T}\).

1.2 Evaluating compound propositions

    1.2: Evaluating compound propositions

1.1 Propositions and logical operations
1.2 Evaluating compound propositions
Learn to:

    Determine truth values for compound propositions given truth values for variables.
    Complete a truth table for two, three, or four variables.
    Use a truth table with intermediate columns to find truth values for complicated propositions.
    Evaluate compound propositions using order of operations for the logical operators of negation, conjunction, and disjunction.
    Translate English statements into statements with logic symbols.

A compound proposition can be created by using more than one operation. For example, the proposition evaluates to true if is true or the negation of is true.

The order in which the operations are applied in a compound proposition such as may affect the truth value of the proposition. Without parentheses, the rule is that negation is applied first, then conjunction, then disjunction:
Figure 1.2.1: Order of operations without parentheses.

    (not)
    (and)
    (or)

Feedback?

For example, the proposition should be read as instead of But good practice is to use parentheses to specify the order in which the operations are to be performed, as in One exception to using parentheses in compound propositions is with the negation operation. Parentheses around are usually omitted to make compound propositions more readable. Because the negation operation is always applied first, the proposition is evaluated as instead of Also, when there are multiple operations or multiple operations, such as in the compound proposition or the compound proposition , parentheses are usually omitted because the order in which the operations are applied does not affect the final truth value. The exclusive or (denoted by ) is not included in the table above because this material does not use the exclusive or operations in compound logical expressions.
participation activity
1.2.1: Evaluating compound propositions.
Start
Static figure: The variables \(p\), \(q\), and \(r\) have the following truth values: \(p\) is true, \(q\) is false, \(r\) is true. There are a series of propositions with underlined parts. The underlined parts of each proposition indicate which operation is the next to be evaluated.
\(p \land \neg(q \lor r)\)
\(\text{T} \land \neg\) begin underline \((\text{F} \lor \text{T})\) end underline
\(\text{T} \land \) begin underline \(\neg \text{T}\) end underline
Begin underline \(\text{T} \land \text{F}\) end underline
\(\text{F}\)
Step 1: The compound proposition \(p \land \neg(q \lor r)\) is evaluated by filling in the truth values for variables \(p\), \(q\), and \(r .\) The variables and their truth assignments are revealed: \(p: \text{T}\), \(q: \text{F}\), \(r: \text{T} .\) The proposition \(p \land \neg(q \lor r)\) is shown. On the line below, the proposition is duplicated, and then each variable is replaced with its truth value. The new proposition reads \(\text{T} \land \neg (\text{F} \lor \text{T}) .\) Step 2: Next, operations are evaluated in the required order. The part of the proposition in parentheses, \((\text{F} \lor \text{T})\), is underlined. On a new line, the proposition \(\text{T} \land \neg (\text{F} \lor \text{T})\) is duplicated. However, the underlined part, \((\text{F} \lor \text{T})\), is evaluated to be true and so a \(\text{T}\) replaces \((\text{F} \lor \text{T}) .\) The new proposition reads \(\text{T} \land \neg \text{T} .\) Step 3: The compound proposition \(p \land \neg(q \lor r)\) evaluates to false. In the proposition \(\text{T} \land \neg \text{T}\), the part \(\neg \text{T}\) is underlined and evaluated to be false. \(\neg \text{T}\) is replaced with an \(\text{F}\) so the new proposition is \(\text{T} \land \text{F}\) The proposition \(\text{T} \land \text{F}\) is evaluated to be false and an \(\text{F}\) is written on a new line.

Filling in the rows of a truth table

A truth table for a compound proposition has a row for every possible combination of truth assignments for the statement's variables. If a compound proposition has variables, there are rows. The truth table for compound proposition has rows. The last square in each row shows the truth value for the compound proposition when the variables are set according to the truth values in that row.

Each column is labeled. The columns corresponding to single variables are to the left, and the column with the compound propositions is to the right. The truth values in the variable columns are chosen methodically to create all possible combinations.

To fill in the variable columns, each column is filled in from top to bottom, beginning with Start with the rightmost variable column and fill in the squares with an alternating and pattern. The next column to the left is filled in by an alternating and pattern. The next column to the left is filled in by an alternating and pattern. For each new column, the number of s and s in the pattern is doubled.
participation activity
1.2.3: How to fill in a truth table.
Start
Static figure: The compound proposition \((p \lor r) \land \neg q\) is in the top left corner. Below the proposition is the following truth table.
\(p\) 	\(q\) 	\(r\) 	\((p \lor r) \land \neg q\)
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\)
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\)
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\)
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\)
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\)
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\)
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\)
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\)
Step 1: A column for each variable and a column for the compound proposition \((p \lor r) \land \neg q .\) There are three variables, so there are \(2^3 = 8\) rows. The proposition \((p \lor r) \land \neg q\) is shown, and then an empty \(4\) by \(9\) table appears. The header row is filled in with the variables \(p\), \(q\), and \(r\) as the headers for the first \(3\) columns and the proposition \((p \lor r) \land \neg q\) as the header for the final column. Step 2: The rightmost variable column is filled in with alternating \(\text{T}\)s and \(\text{F}\)s. Moving left, the next variable column alternates by twos. Moving left again, the next variable column alternates by fours. The \(r\) column is filled in with alternating \(\text{T}\)s and \(\text{F}\)s. Next, the \(q\) column is filled in by alternating with \(2\) \(\text{T}\)s and then \(2\) \(\text{F}\)s. Finally, the \(p\) column is filled in with \(4\) \(\text{T}\)s and then \(4\) \(\text{F}\)s. Step 3: The column with the compound proposition shows the truth value for each truth assignment to the variables. The proposition column is filled in with \(\text{T}\)s and \(\text{F}\)s based on the truth value of the proposition for the truth assignment of the row. For example, \((p \lor r) \land \neg q = \text{F}\) when \(p = \text{F}\), \(q = \text{T}\), and \(r= \text{F}\) .

participation activity
1.2.6: Truth table with intermediate columns.
Start
Static figure: The proposition \(\neg q \land (p \lor r)\) is in the top left. Next to it is the label "Compound proposition." Below the proposition is the following truth table.
\(p\) 	\(q\) 	\(r\) 	\(\neg q\) 	\((p \lor r)\) 	\(\neg q \land (p \lor r)\)
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\)
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\)
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\)
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\)
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\)
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\)
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\)
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\)
Step 1: The truth table for \(\neg q \land (p \lor r)\) can be computed by first filling in a column for \(\neg q .\) The variable columns \(p\), \(q\), and \(r\) are filled in. Next, the \(\neg q\) column is filled in using the truth values in the \(q\) column. Step 2: Then a column for \((p \lor r)\) is filled in. The \((p \lor r)\) column is filled in by using the truth values in the \(p\) column and the \(r\) column. Step 3: Finally the column for \(\neg q \land (p \lor r)\) is filled in using the intermediate columns. The \(\neg q \land (p \lor r)\) column is filled in by using the truth values in the \(\neg q\) column and the \((p \lor r)\) column.

Example 1.2.1: Logic in electronic devices.

Most of the devices and appliances used in everyday life are controlled by electronic circuitry that works according to the laws of logic. A designer of an electronically controlled device must first express the desired behavior in logic, and then test that the device behaves as desired under every set of circumstances.

An electronic fan automatically turns on and off depending on the humidity in a room. Since the humidity detector is not very accurate, the fan stays on for minutes once triggered to clear the room of moisture. A manual off switch can also be used to override the automatic functioning of the control.

Define the following propositions:

    The fan has been on for twenty minutes.
    The humidity level in the room is low.
    The manual "off" button has been pushed.

When the fan is on, the fan turns off if the following proposition evaluates to true:

A truth table is useful in testing the device to make sure it works as intended under every set of circumstances. The following table might be used by a technician testing the electronic fan.
			Should be off? ( yes)

1.3 Conditional statements

1.2 Evaluating compound propositions
1.3 Conditional statements
Learn to:

    Translate English statements into statements with logic symbols.
    Express equivalent conditional expressions in English.
    Demonstrate the converse, inverse, and contrapositive of a proposition.
    Evaluate compound propositions with conditional and biconditional operations.
    Complete a truth table for conditional and biconditional propositions.
    Determine if a truth value of a compound expression is known given a partial truth assignment.

The conditional operation is denoted with the symbol The proposition is read "if , then ." The proposition is false if is true and is false; otherwise, is true.

A compound proposition that uses a conditional operation is called a conditional proposition. A conditional proposition expressed in English is sometimes referred to as a conditional statement, as in "If there is a traffic jam today, then I will be late for work."

In , the proposition is called the hypothesis, and the proposition is called the conclusion. The truth table for is given below.
Table 1.3.1: Truth table for the conditional operation.
		
		
		
		
		
Feedback?

A conditional proposition is like a contract between two parties, as in:

If you mow Mr. Smith's lawn, then he will pay you.

The only way for the contract between you and Mr. Smith to be broken is for you to mow Mr. Smith's lawn and for him not to pay you. If you do not mow his lawn, then he can either pay you or not, and the contract is not broken. In the words of logic, the only way for a conditional statement to be false is if the hypothesis is true and the conclusion is false. If the hypothesis is false, then the conditional statement is true regardless of the truth value of the conclusion.
Figure 1.3.1: A conditional statement illustrated.
A truth table for the conditional statement If you mow Mr. Smith's lawn, then he will pay you where p represents mowing the lawn and q represents Mr. Smith paying you. The conditional statement is true for three scenarios: when both p and q are true, when p is false and q is true, and when p and q are both false. The only scenario in which the contract is broken is when p is true and q is false.
Feedback?
participation activity
1.3.1: Understanding conditional statements.

Each question has a proposition that is a conditional statement. Truth values are also given for the individual propositions contained in that conditional statement. Indicate whether the conditional statement is true or false.
1)

It is raining today.

2)

Sally did not take too long getting ready.
Sally missed the bus.

It is not sunny out.
I am not riding my bike.

There are many ways to express the conditional statement in English:
Table 1.3.2: English expressions of the conditional operation.

Consider the propositions:

    You mow Mr. Smith's lawn.
    Mr. Smith will pay you.

If , then 	If you mow Mr. Smith's lawn, then he will pay you.
If , 	If you mow Mr. Smith's lawn, he will pay you.
if 	Mr. Smith will pay you if you mow his lawn.
implies 	Mowing Mr. Smith's lawn implies that he will pay you.
whenever 	Mr. Smith will pay you whenever you mow his lawn.
only if 	You will mow Mr. Smith's lawn only if he pays you.
is sufficient for 	Mowing Mr. Smith's lawn is sufficient for him to pay you.
is necessary for 	Mr. Smith's paying you is necessary for you to mow his lawn.

The fact that the statement " only if " is the same as the proposition can be confusing. Both statements mean that the only way for to be true is if is also true.

The biconditional operation

If and are propositions, the proposition " if and only if " is expressed with the biconditional operation and is denoted The proposition is true when and have the same truth value and is false when and have different truth values.

Compound propositions with conditional and biconditional operations

The conditional and biconditional operations can be combined with other logical operations, as in If parentheses are not used to explicitly indicate the order in which to apply the operations, then , , and is applied before or Thus, the proposition is evaluated as A good practice is to use parentheses so that the order of operations is clear.

1.3.4: Example of evaluating a compound proposition with a biconditional.
Start
Static figure: The variables \(p\), \(q\), and\(r\) have the following truth values: \(p\) is true, \(q\) is true, \(r\) is false. There are a series of propositions with parts underlined. The parts that are underlined signify the next part of the proposition that is to be evaluated.
\(p \lor \neg( q \leftrightarrow r)\)
\(\text{T} \lor \neg\) begin underline \(( \text{T} \leftrightarrow \text{F})\) end underline
\(\text{T} \lor \) begin underline\( \neg\text{F}\) end underline
begin underline \(\text{T} \lor \text{T}\) end underline
\(\text{T}\)
Below the table is the text \(p \lor \neg( q \leftrightarrow r)\) is true. Step 1: The compound proposition, \(p \lor \neg(q \leftrightarrow r)\) is evaluated by first filling in the given truth values for \(p\), \(q\) and r. The animation begins with the proposition \(p \lor \neg( q \leftrightarrow r)\) and the truth assignments of \(p\), \(q\), and \(r\) visible, \(p\colon \text{T}\), \(q\colon \text{T}\), \(r\colon \text{F}.\) On the line below the proposition is duplicated and then each variable is replaced with its truth value. The new line now reads \(\text{T} \lor \neg (\text{T} \leftrightarrow \text{F}) .\) Step 2: The biconditional operation is evaluated first. The \((\text{T} \leftrightarrow \text{F})\) is underlined in the proposition \(\text{T} \lor \neg (\text{T} \leftrightarrow \text{F}) .\) The biconditional is evaluated to be F. On a new line the proposition is duplicated. However, an \(\text{F}\) replaces the biconditional. The new line now reads \(\text{T} \lor \neg\text{F} .\) Step 3: Next, the negation and then the disjunction are evaluated, yielding a truth value for the entire compound proposition. The \((\neg \text{F})\) is underlined in the proposition \(\text{T} \lor \neg\text{F} .\) The negation is evaluated to be true. A new line reads \(\text{T} \lor \text{T} .\) The new proposition \(\text{T} \lor \text{T}\) is underlined. The disjunction is evaluated to be \(\text{T}\). A \(\text{T}\) is written on a new line.

1.4 Logical equivalence
Learn to:

    Determine if a compound proposition is a tautology or contradiction.
    Show that two compound propositions are logically equivalent using a truth table.
    Show that two compound propositions are not logically equivalent using a specific truth assignment.
    Apply De Morgan's laws for negating the conjunction and disjunction of two propositional variables.
    Rewrite English expressions using De Morgan's laws.

A compound proposition is a tautology if the proposition is always true, regardless of the truth value of the individual propositions that occur in it. A compound proposition is a contradiction if the proposition is always false, regardless of the truth value of the individual propositions that occur in it. The proposition is a simple example of a tautology since the proposition is always true whether is true or false. The fact that is a tautology can be verified in a truth table, which shows that every truth value in the rightmost column is true.
Table 1.4.1: Truth table for tautology .
		
		
		
Feedback?

Similarly, the proposition is an example of a simple contradiction, because the proposition is false regardless of whether is true or false. The truth table below shows that is a contradiction because every truth value in the rightmost column is false.
Table 1.4.2: Truth table for contradiction .
		
		
		
Feedback?

Showing that a compound proposition is not a tautology only requires showing a particular set of truth values for its individual propositions that cause the compound proposition to evaluate to false. For example, the proposition is not a tautology because when and then is false. Showing that a compound proposition is not a contradiction only requires showing a particular set of truth values for its individual propositions that cause the compound proposition to evaluate to true. For example, the proposition is not a contradiction because when then is true.

Showing logical equivalence using truth tables

Two compound propositions are logically equivalent if they have the same truth value regardless of the truth values of their individual propositions. If and are two compound propositions, the notation is used to indicate that and are logically equivalent. For example, and have the same truth value regardless of whether is true or false, so Propositions and are logically equivalent if and only if the proposition is a tautology. Note that if and only if

A truth table can be used to show that two compound propositions are logically equivalent.

1.4.2: Showing with a truth table.
Start
Static figure: The following table is on the left side.
\(p\) 	\(\neg p\) 	\(p \rightarrow \neg p\)
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\)
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\)
A box is drawn around the two \(\text{F}\)s next to each other in the \(\neg p\) and \(p \rightarrow \neg p\) columns. A box is also drawn around the two \(\text{T}\)s next to each other in the \(\neg p\) and \(p \rightarrow \neg p\) columns. Next to the table is the following text. \(\neg p\) and \(p \rightarrow \neg p\) have the same truth values, so \(\neg p \equiv p \rightarrow \neg p .\) Step 1: \(p \to \neg p\) can be shown to be equivalent to \(\neg p\) by filling in a column for \(\neg p .\) The \(\neg p\) column is evaluated based on the \(p\) column. Step 2: Then a column for \(p\to\neg p\) is filled in, and the two columns are verified to be the same. The \(p\to\neg p\) column is evaluated based on both the \(p\) column and the \(\neg p\) column. The \(p\to\neg p\) column has the same truth values as the \(\neg p\) column.

De Morgan's laws

De Morgan's laws are logical equivalences that show how to correctly distribute a negation operation inside a parenthesized expression. Both versions of De Morgan's laws are particularly useful in logical reasoning. The first De Morgan's law is:

When the negation operation is distributed inside the parentheses, the disjunction operation changes to a conjunction operation. Consider an English example with the following propositions for and

    The patient has migraines
    The patient has high blood pressure

The use of the English word "or" throughout the example is assumed to be disjunction (that is, the inclusive or). De Morgan's law says that the following two English statements are logically equivalent:
It is not true that the patient has migraines or high blood pressure.
The patient does not have migraines and does not have high blood pressure.

The logical equivalence can be verified using a truth table. Alternatively, reasoning about which truth assignments cause the expressions and to evaluate to true provides intuition about why the two expressions are logically equivalent.
participation activity
1.4.5: Reasoning about De Morgan's laws.
1)
There is only one truth assignment for and that makes the expression evaluate to true. Which one is it?
2)
There is only one truth assignment for and that makes the expression evaluate to true. Which one is it?
Feedback?

The second version of De Morgan's law swaps the role of the disjunction and conjunction:

Continuing with the same example, the following two statements are logically equivalent:
It is not true that the patient has migraines and high blood pressure.
The patient does not have migraines or does not have high blood pressure.

The logical equivalence can be verified using a truth table. Alternatively, reasoning about which truth assignments cause the expressions and to evaluate to false provides intuition about why the two expressions are logically equivalent.

1.5 Laws of propositional logic
Learn to:

    Simplify a compound proposition using the laws of propositional logic.
    Write a proof using the laws of propositional logic to show that two compound propositions are logically equivalent.

If two propositions are logically equivalent, then one can be substituted for the other within a more complex proposition. The compound proposition after the substitution is logically equivalent to the compound proposition before the substitution.

For example Therefore,

Using the laws of propositional logic to show logical equivalence

Substitution gives an alternate way of showing that two propositions are logically equivalent. If one proposition can be obtained from another by a series of substitutions using equivalent expressions, then the two propositions are logically equivalent. The table below shows several laws of propositional logic that are particularly useful for establishing the logical equivalence of compound propositions:
Table 1.5.1: Laws of propositional logic.
Idempotent laws:

Commutative laws:

1.5.3: The laws of propositional logic can be used to show logical equivalence.
Start
Static figure: The equivalence \(p \rightarrow q \equiv \neg q \rightarrow \neg p\) is underlined. A sequence of equivalent propositions are shown in the table below.
\((p \rightarrow q)\) 	Original proposition
\((\neg p \lor q)\) 	Conditional identity
\((q \lor \neg p)\) 	Commutative law
\((\neg \neg q \lor \neg p)\) 	Double negation law
\((\neg q \rightarrow \neg p)\) 	Conditional identity
Step 1: The proof that \(p \rightarrow q\) is equivalent to \(\neg q\rightarrow \neg p\) starts with \(p \rightarrow q\) and applies the conditional identity to get \(\neg p \lor q.\) Step 2: The commutative law is applied to get \(q \lor \neg p.\) Step 3: The double negation law is applied to get \(\neg \neg q \lor \neg p.\) The double negation law is applied to the \(q\) in \(q \lor \neg p .\) \(q\) is equivalent to \(\neg \neg q .\) Step 4: Finally, the conditional identity is applied again to get \(\neg q \rightarrow \neg p.\)
Captions
Feedback?
participation activity
1.5.4: The laws of propositional logic can be used to simplify compound propositions.
Start
Static figure: The equivalence \(\neg(p\lor q) \lor (\neg p \land q) \equiv \neg p\) is underlined. A sequence of equivalent propositions are shown in the table below.
\(\neg(p\lor q) \lor (\neg p \land q)\) 	Original proposition
\((\neg p \land \neg q) \lor (\neg p \land q)\) 	De Morgan's law
\(\neg p \land (\neg q \lor q)\) 	Distributive law
\(\neg p \land (q \lor \neg q)\) 	Commutative law
\(\neg p \land \text{T}\) 	Complement law
\(\neg p\) 	Identity law
Step 1: The proof that \(\neg (p \lor q) \lor (\neg p \land q)\) is equivalent to \(\neg p\) starts with \(\neg (p \lor q) \lor (\neg p \land q)\) and applies De Morgan's law to get \((\neg p \land \neg q) \lor (\neg p \land q).\) De Morgan's law is applied to the disjunction \(\neg(p\lor q)\) to get the conjunction \((\neg p \land \neg q) .\) Step 2: The Distributive law is applied to get \(\neg p \land (\neg q \lor q).\) Step 3: The Commutative law is applied to get \(\neg p \land (q \lor \neg q).\) The Commutative law is applied to the disjunction \((\neg q \lor q)\) to rearrange the terms to \((q \lor \neg q) .\) Step 4: The Complement law is applied to get \(\neg p \land \text{T}.\) The Complement law is applied to the disjunction \((q \lor \neg q)\) which evaluates to \(\text{T}.\) Step 5: Finally, the Identity law is applied again to get \(\neg p.\)
Captions
Feedback?

1.6 Predicates and quantifiers

    1.6: Predicates and quantifiers

1.5 Laws of propositional logic
1.6 Predicates and quantifiers
Learn to:

    Differentiate between propositions and predicates.
    Determine the truth value of a quantified statement for a given set of predicates and domains.
    Translate a mathematical statement into an equivalent logical expression using predicates and quantifiers.
    Understand the requirements to show that a quantified statement is true or false..
    Provide an example to show an existentially quantified statement is true.
    Provide a counterexample to show a universally quantified statement is false..

Many mathematical statements contain variables. The statement " is an odd number" is not a proposition because the statement does not have a well-defined truth value until the value of is specified. If , the statement is true. If , the statement is false. The truth value of the statement can be expressed as a function of the variable , as in . The expression is read " of ." A logical statement with a truth value that is a function of one or more variables is called a predicate. If is defined to be the statement " is an odd number," then corresponds to the statement " is an odd number." is a proposition because it has a well-defined truth value.

A predicate can depend on more than one variable. Define the predicates and as:


The proposition is true because The proposition is false because

The domain of a variable in a predicate is the set of all possible values for the variable. For example, a natural domain for the variable in the predicate " is an odd number" is the set of all integers. If the domain of a variable in a predicate is not clear from context, the domain should be given as part of the definition of the predicate.

Statements outside the realm of mathematics can also be predicates. For example, consider the statement: "The city has a population over " The "city" is the variable and the domain is defined to be the set of all cities in the United States. When the city is New York, the statement becomes: "New York has a population over " and the statement is true. When the city is Toledo, the statement becomes: "Toledo has a population over " and the statement is false.

A statement may be true for all values in the domain. But if the statement contains a variable, the statement is still considered a predicate and not a proposition. For example, if is the statement "" and the domain is all positive integers, the statement is true for each value in the domain. But is considered a predicate and not a proposition because it contains a variable.

Universal quantifier

If all the variables in a predicate are assigned specific values from their domains, then the predicate becomes a proposition with a well-defined truth value. Another way to turn a predicate into a proposition is to use a quantifier. The logical statement is read "for all " or "for every ." The statement asserts that is true for every possible value of in its domain. The symbol is a universal quantifier, and the statement is a universally quantified statement. is a proposition because it is either true or false. is true if and only if is true for every in the domain of variable

If the domain is a finite set of elements then the equality below holds for any predicate defined on the domain:

If the domain is the set of students in a class and the predicate means that student completed the assignment, then the proposition means: "Every student completed the assignment." Establishing that is true requires showing that every student in the class did complete the assignment.

Universally quantified statements can be proven to be true by showing that the predicate holds for an arbitrary element from the domain. An arbitrary element means nothing is assumed about the element other than the fact that it is in the domain. In the following example, the domain is the set of all positive integers:

The statement is true because when is assigned any arbitrary value from the set of all positive integers, the inequality holds.
participation activity
1.6.3: Proving is true for an arbitrary positive integer .
Start
Static figure: Text: \(\forall x, \frac{1}{x+1} \lt 1 .\)
\(0 \lt x\) 	True for all positive integers x
\(1 \lt x + 1\) 	Add 1 to both sides
\(\frac{1}{x+1} \lt \frac{x + 1}{x+1}\) 	Divide both sides by \(x + 1\)
\(\forall x, \frac{1}{x+1} \lt 1\) 	Is true for all positive integers x
Step 1: The proof starts with the fact that \(0 \lt x\) for all positive integers and adds 1 to both sides to get \(1 \lt 1 + x .\) Step 2: Dividing both sides by \((x + 1)\) gives that \(\frac{1}{(x + 1)} \lt 1 .\) The inequality is true for all positive integers x.
Captions
Feedback?

A counterexample for a universally quantified statement is an element in the domain for which the predicate is false. A single counterexample is sufficient to show that a universally quantified statement is false. For example, consider the statement in which the domain is the set of positive integers. When then and the statement is not true. Therefore, is a counterexample that shows the statement "" is false.

Note that it is typically assumed that the domain contains at least one element. If the domain for variable is empty, then the statement is true because there are no elements in the domain for which is false.

Existential quantifier

The logical statement is read "there exists an , such that P(x)." The statement asserts that is true for at least one possible value for in its domain. The symbol is an existential quantifier, and the statement is called an existentially quantified statement. is a proposition because it is either true or false. is true if and only if is true for at least one value in the domain of variable

If the domain is a finite set of elements then the following equality holds for any predicate defined on the domain:

If the domain is the set of students in a class and the predicate means that student completed the assignment, then is the statement: "There is a student who completed the assignment." Establishing that is true only requires finding one particular student who completed the assignment. An example for an existentially quantified statement is an element in the domain for which the predicate is true. A single example is sufficient to show that an existentially quantified statement is true. But showing that is false requires showing that every student in the class did not complete the assignment.

Some existentially quantified statements can be shown to be false by showing that the predicate is false for an arbitrary element from the domain. For example, consider the existentially quantified statement in which the domain of is the set of all positive integers:

The statement is false because no positive integer satisfies the expression

Note that it is typically assumed that the domain contains at least one element. If the domain for variable is empty, then the statement is false because there is no element in the domain for which is true.

1.6.5: Showing is false.
Start
Static figure: Text: \(\exists x, x +1 \lt x .\)
\(x+1 \lt x\) 	Subtract x
\(1 \nless 0\) 	This inequality does not hold regardless of the value of x
\(\exists x, x +1 \lt x\) 	Is false
Step 1: The proof starts with the inequality \(x + 1 \lt x\) and subtracts \(x\) from both sides to get \(1 \lt 0 .\) Step 2: Since \(1 \lt 0\) is false, the inequality \(x + 1 \lt x\) is false for every \(x\) and therefore \(\exists x (x + 1 \lt x)\) is false.

1.6 Predicates and quantifiers
1.7 Quantified statements
Learn to:

    Translate between English statements and logical expressions involving quantifiers, predicates, and logical operators.
    Determine the truth value of a quantified statement with logical operators given a table for the predicates over a finite domain.

Universally and existentially quantified statements can also be constructed from logical operations. Consider an example in which the domain is the set of positive integers and define the following predicates:

    is prime

    is odd

The proposition states that there exists a positive number that is prime and not odd. This proposition is true because of the example Since is prime and not odd, the statement is true.

The proposition says that for every positive integer , if is prime, then is odd. This proposition is false because of the counterexample Since is prime and not odd, the conditional statement is false.

The universal and existential quantifiers are generically called quantifiers. A logical statement that includes a universal or existential quantifier is called a quantified statement. The quantifiers and are applied before the logical operations (, , , and ) used for propositions. This means that the statement is equivalent to as opposed to .

A variable in the predicate is called a free variable because the variable is free to take on any value in the domain. The variable in the statement is a bound variable because the variable is bound to a quantifier. A statement with no free variables is a proposition because the statement's truth value can be determined.

The scope of a quantifier is the part of the logical expression to which the quantifier applies. In the expression , the scope of the quantifier includes the predicate but not Therefore, the variable in is bound by the universal quantifier, but the variable in is not bound by the universal quantifier. Since the expression has a free variable, the expression is not a proposition. In contrast, the scope of the universal quantifier in the statement includes all of Therefore, the quantifier binds both occurrences of the variable and the statement is a proposition.

Variable names

Consider the predicate which has one input variable. The variable name can be exchanged with other variable names, such as or as long as the variables are used consistently. If a variable is bound by a quantifier, a different variable name can be used, as long as the substitution is done throughout the scope of the quantifier. Therefore the following statements all have the same meaning:




A variable name can be reused, as long as the scopes of the quantifiers that bind the variables do not overlap. So the statement is valid. The scope of the first quantifier includes the predicate and the scope of the second quantifier includes the variable .

Translating between English statements and quantified statements

    came to the party

    was sick

The statement "Everyone was not sick" has the same meaning as "" because the two statements have the same truth value regardless of who was invited to the party and whether they were sick.

1.7 Quantified statements
1.8 De Morgan's law for quantified statements
Learn to:

    Simplify universally and existentially quantified statements using De Morgan's law.
    Use De Morgan's law for quantified statements to prove logical equivalence.
    Determine the negation of a quantified statement by applying De Morgan's law.
    Apply De Morgan's law for quantified statements to English statements.

De Morgan's law for quantified statements

The negation operation can be applied to a quantified statement, such as If the domain for the variable is the set of all birds and the predicate is " can fly," then the statement is equivalent to:

"Not every bird can fly."

which is logically equivalent to the statement:

"There exists a bird that cannot fly."

Note that logical equivalence for quantified statements means that the statements have the same truth value for any sets used as the domains of the variables and any predicates defined on those domains.

The equivalence of the previous two statements is an example of De Morgan's law for quantified statements, which is formally stated as The diagram below illustrates that for a finite domain, De Morgan's law for universally quantified statements corresponds to the De Morgan's law for propositions:
Figure 1.8.1: De Morgan's law for universally quantified statements.


Thus,
Feedback?

Similarly, consider the statement in which the domain is the set of children enrolled in a class and is the predicate " is absent today." The statement is expressed in English as:

"It is not true that there is a child in the class who is absent today."

which is logically equivalent to:

"Every child enrolled in the class is not absent today."

The logical equivalence of the last two statements is an example of the second of De Morgan's laws for quantified statements: The diagram below illustrates that for a finite domain, De Morgan's law for existentially quantified statements is the same as De Morgan's law for propositions:
Figure 1.8.2: De Morgan's law for existentially quantified statements.

Table 1.8.1: Summary of De Morgan's laws for quantified statements.
Feedback?
participation activity
1.8.1: De Morgan's law can be used to simplify an existentially quantified statement.
Start
Static figure: Table:
\(\lnot \exists x (P(x) \rightarrow \lnot Q(x))\) 	
\(\forall x \lnot (P(x) \rightarrow \lnot Q(x))\) 	De Morgan's law
\(\forall x \lnot (\lnot P(x) \lor \lnot Q(x))\) 	Conditional identity
\(\forall x (\lnot \lnot P(x) \land \lnot \lnot Q(x))\) 	De Morgan's law
\(\forall x ( P(x) \land Q(x))\) 	Double negation law
Step 1: Start with \(\lnot\exists x(P(x) \to \lnot Q(x))\) and apply De Morgan's law to get \(\forall x \lnot(P(x) \to \lnot Q(x))\). Step 2: Then apply the conditional identity to get \(\forall x \lnot(\lnot P(x) \lor \lnot Q(x))\). The conditional identity is applied to conditional \(P(x) \to \lnot Q(x)\) to get \(\lnot P(x) \lor \lnot Q(x)\). Step 3: Then apply De Morgan's law to get \(\forall x (\lnot\lnot P(x) \land \lnot\lnot Q(x))\). De Morgan's law is applied to the negated disjunction \(\lnot(\lnot P(x) \lor \lnot Q(x))\) to get \((\lnot\lnot P(x) \land \lnot\lnot Q(x))\). Step 4: Finally, apply the double negation law to get \(\forall x (P(x) \land Q(x))\). The double negation law is applied twice to each \(\lnot \lnot P(x)\) and \(\lnot \lnot Q(x)\).

1.8.2: De Morgan's law can be used to simplify a universally quantified statement.
Start
Static figure: Table:
\(\lnot \forall x (P(x) \land \lnot Q(x))\) 	
\(\exists x \lnot (P(x) \land \lnot Q(x))\) 	De Morgan's law
\(\exists x (\lnot P(x) \lor \lnot \lnot Q(x))\) 	De Morgan's law
\(\exists x ( \lnot P(x) \lor Q(x))\) 	Double negation law
Step 1: Start with \(\lnot\forall x(P(x) \land \lnot Q(x))\) and apply De Morgan's law to get \(\exists x \lnot (P(x) \land \lnot Q(x))\). Step 2: Then apply De Morgan's law to get \(\exists x (\lnot P(x) \lor \lnot\lnot Q(x))\). De Morgan's law is applied to the negated conjunction \( \lnot (P(x) \land \lnot Q(x))\) to get \((\lnot P(x) \lor \lnot\lnot Q(x))\). Step 3: Finally, apply the double negation law to get \(\exists x (\lnot P(x) \lor Q(x))\). The Double negation law is applied to \(\lnot \lnot Q(x)\) to get \(Q(x)\).

1.8 De Morgan's law for quantified statements
1.9 Nested quantifiers
Learn to:

    Identify the free and bound variables in a logical expression with nested quantifiers.
    Determine if a logical expression with nested quantifiers is a proposition.
    Evaluate the truth value of logical expressions with nested quantifiers.
    Apply De Morgan's law to statements with nested quantifiers.

If a predicate has more than one variable, each variable must be bound by a separate quantifier. A logical expression with more than one quantifier that binds different variables in the same predicate has nested quantifiers. The examples below show several logical expressions and which variables are bound in each. The logical expression is a proposition if all the variables are bound.
Figure 1.9.1: Quantifiers and bound variables.

Nested quantifiers of the same type

Consider a scenario where the domain is a group of people working on a joint project. Define the predicate to be:

sent an email to

and consider the proposition: The proposition can be expressed in English as:

"Everyone sent an email to everyone."

The statement is true if is true for every pair, and . The universal quantifiers include the case that , so if is true, then everyone sent an email to everyone else and everyone sent an email to themselves. The statement is false if there is any pair, and , that causes to be false. In particular, is false even if there is a single individual who did not send themselves an email.

Now consider the proposition: The proposition can be expressed in English as:

"There is a person who sent an email to someone."

The statement is true if there is a pair, and , in the domain that causes to evaluate to true. In particular, is true even in the situation that there is a single individual who sent an email to themselves. The statement is false if all pairs, and , cause to evaluate to false.

Alternating nested quantifiers

A quantified expression can contain both types of quantifiers as in: The quantifiers are applied from left to right, so the statement translates into English as:

"There is a person who sent an email to everyone."

Switching the quantifiers changes the meaning of the proposition:

"Every person sent an email to someone."

In reasoning whether a quantified statement is true or false, it is useful to think of the statement as a two-player game in which two players compete to set the statement's truth value. One of the players is the "existential player" and the other player is the "universal player." The variables are set from left to right in the expression. The table below summarizes which variables are set by which player and the goal of each player:
Table 1.9.1: Nested quantifiers as a two-player game.
Player 	Action 	Goal
Existential player 	Selects values for existentially bound variables 	Tries to make the expression true
Universal player 	Selects values for universally bound variables 	Tries to make the expression false
Feedback?

If the predicate is true after all the variables are set, then the quantified statement is true. If the predicate is false after all the variables are set, then the quantified statement is false. Consider the following quantified statement in which the domain is the set of all integers: The universal player first selects the value of Regardless of which value the universal player selects for , the existential player can select to be , which causes the sum to be . Because the existential player can always succeed in causing the predicate to be true, the statement is true.

Switching the order of the quantifiers gives the following statement: Now the existential player goes first and selects a value for Regardless of the value chosen for , the universal player can select some value for that causes the predicate to be false. For example, if is an integer, then is also an integer and Thus, the universal player can always win, and the statement is false.
participation activity
1.9.3: Examples showing reasoning about nested quantifiers using two-player games.
Start
Static figure: Two examples are shown of reasoning about nested quantifiers using two-player games where each player takes on the role of one of the quantifiers. Step 1: Is \(\forall x \exists y (y^2 = x)\) true? The domain is the set of integers. If the universal player selects \(x=13\) (not a perfect square), then the existential player cannot find an integer \(y\) such that \(x=y^2\). Step 2: The universal player wins, so \(\forall x \exists y (y^2 = x)\) is false. Step 3: Is \(\exists x \forall y (x + y = y)\) true? If the existential player selects \(x=0\), then for any \(y\) that the universal player selects, \(0 + y = y\). Step 4: The existential player wins, so \(\exists x \forall y (x+y=y)\) is true.

Nested quantifiers with more than two variables

Logical expressions with nested quantifiers can have more than two variables. The example below has three variables that are bound by three different quantifiers. The quantifiers are read from left to right. So the logical expression above is the statement: "For every and every , there is a , such that ". If the domain for all three variables is the set of integers, then the statement is true since the sum of every two integers is also an integer. Regardless of how the universal player selects and , the existential player can select to be .

The order is important in expressions with more than one quantifier. The logical expression translates in English to the statement "There is a , such that for every and every , ". The statement is false because once and are chosen, the universal player can select to be any value except , which makes the statement false.

Note that two quantifiers of the same type that are next to each other in a logical expression can be switched to obtain a logically equivalent expression. For example:

De Morgan's law with nested quantifiers

De Morgan's law can be applied to logical statements with more than one quantifier. Each time the negation operation moves past a quantifier, the quantifier changes type from universal to existential or from existential to universal:
Table 1.9.2: De Morgan's laws for nested quantified statements.
Feedback?

Consider a scenario in which the domain is the set of all students in a school. The predicate indicates that likes The statement is read as:

There is a student who likes everyone in the school.

The negation of the statement is:

There is no student who likes everyone in the school.

Applying De Morgan's laws yields: which translates to:

1.9.7: De Morgan's law for nested quantifiers.
Start
Static figure: Sequential applications of De Morgan's law are shown for the four different orders of nested quantifiers: \(\lnot \forall x \forall y P(x,y) \equiv \exists x \lnot \forall y P(x,y) \equiv \exists x \exists y \lnot P(x,y)\) \(\lnot \exists x \forall y P(x,y) \equiv \forall x \lnot \forall y P(x,y) \equiv \forall x \exists y \lnot P(x,y)\) \(\lnot \forall x \exists y P(x,y) \equiv \exists x \lnot \exists y P(x,y) \equiv \exists x \forall y \lnot P(x,y)\) \(\lnot \exists x \exists y P(x,y) \equiv \forall x \lnot \forall y P(x,y) \equiv \forall x \forall y \lnot P(x,y)\) Step 1: If De Morgan's law is applied once to \(\lnot\forall x \forall y P(x, y)\), the result is \(\exists x \lnot\forall y P(x,y)\). Step 2: Applying De Morgan's law again gives \(\exists x \exists y \lnot P(x, y)\). Step 3: Applying De Morgan's law once to \(\lnot\forall x \exists y P(x, y)\) gives \(\exists x \lnot\exists y P(x, y)\). The second application gives \(\exists x \forall y \lnot P(x, y)\). Step 4: Applying De Morgan's law once to \(\lnot\exists x \forall y P(x, y)\) gives \(\forall x \lnot\forall y P(x, y)\). The second application gives \(\forall x \exists y \lnot P(x, y)\). Step 5: Applying De Morgan's law once to \(\lnot\exists x \exists y P(x, y)\) gives \(\forall x \lnot\exists y P(x, y)\). The second application gives \(\forall x \forall y \lnot P(x, y)\).

1.9 Nested quantifiers
1.10 More nested quantified statements
Learn to:

    Translate an English statement with "everyone else" into a quantified logical expression.
    Translate an English statement with "exactly one" into a quantified logical expression.
    Evaluate the truth value of a complicated quantified statement given a table for the predicates over a finite domain.

Using logic to express "everyone else"

Consider a scenario where the domain is a group of people working on a joint project. Define the predicate that indicates whether sent an email to The statement asserts that every person sent an email to every other person and every person sent an email to themselves. How could one use logic to express that everyone sent an email to everyone else without including the case that everyone sent an email to themselves? The idea is to use the conditional operation:

The table below shows a group of four people and the truth value of for each pair. For example, sent an email to (that is, ) because the truth value in the row labeled , and the column labeled is
Figure 1.10.1: Email predicate truth values.
Table of the predicate x sent an email to y where all but M(Agnes, Agnes), M(Fred, Fred), M(Sue, Sue) and M(Marge, Marge) are true. Columns and rows are labeled Agnes, Fred, Sue, Marge. the rows are as follows: TTTT, TFTT, TTTT, TTTF
Feedback?

The statement is false because and are both false. But the statement is true. The statement says that for every pair, and , if and are different people, then sent an email to That is, everyone sent an email to everyone else. The statement is true for the table above because for every pair that is not on the diagonal of the table (that is, for every pair such that ), is true.

Sometimes the meaning of "else" is expressed using the words "other than". The two sentences below have the same meaning and both correspond to the logical expression above.

"Everyone sent an email to everyone else."

"Everyone sent an email to everyone, other than themselves."

Neither statement indicates whether or not there are people who sent an email to themselves. Thus, the logical statement above is true for any setting of the truth values in the table along the diagonal.

Expressing uniqueness in quantified statements

An existentially quantified statement evaluates to true even if there is more than one element in the domain that causes the predicate to evaluate to true. If the domain is a set of people who attend a meeting and the predicate indicates whether or not came late to the meeting, then the statement is true if there are one, two, or more people who came late.

In some cases, it is possible to move quantifiers in an expression to obtain an equivalent expression. Suppose that is an expression that does not contain the variable . Then the following equivalence holds:

The equivalence is an example of a rule in logic called a null qualification rule . The in the predicate indicates that the predicate can depend on other variables, besides . The rule can be applied to the example above in which the predicate indicates that was late to the meeting. It was shown that the statement:

"Exactly one person was late to the meeting"

Moving quantifiers does not always result in an equivalent expression. If the conjunction in the null qualification rule is replaced with the biconditional , then the rule does not hold. For example, the expression is not equivalent to .

1.11 Logical reasoning

    1.11: Logical reasoning

1.10 More nested quantified statements
1.11 Logical reasoning
Learn to:

    Identify the hypothesis and conclusion of a logical argument.
    Determine the validity of a logical argument using a truth table.
    Provide a truth assignment to prove that a logical argument is invalid.
    Translate a logical argument written in English statements to an equivalent argument with logic symbols.

The language of logic allows one to formally establish the truth of logical statements, assuming that a set of hypotheses is true. An argument is a sequence of propositions, called hypotheses, followed by a final proposition, called the conclusion. An argument is valid if the conclusion is true for every truth assignment to the variables that causes all of the hypotheses to be true, otherwise the argument is invalid. An argument is denoted as:





are the hypotheses, and is the conclusion. The symbol reads "therefore." The argument is valid whenever the proposition is a tautology. According to the commutative law, reordering the hypotheses does not change whether an argument is valid or not. Therefore, two arguments are considered to be the same even if the hypotheses appear in a different order. For example, the following two arguments are considered to be the same:





and are the hypotheses. is the conclusion.

One way to establish the validity of an argument is to use a truth table.

1.11.2: Using a truth table to establish the validity of an argument.
Start
Static figure: Argument: \(\begin{align} & p\rightarrow q\\ & p \\ \hline &\therefore q \end{align}\) Truth table:
Hypothesis 	Conclusion 	Hypothesis
\(p\) 	\(q\) 	\(p\rightarrow q\)
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\)
\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\)
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\) 	\(\text{T}\)
\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{F}\) 	\(\text{T}\)
Step 1: To show that the argument with hypotheses \(p\) and \(p \to q\) and conclusion \(q\) is valid, fill in the truth table for \(p, q\), and \(p \to q\). Step 2: There is only one row in which both hypotheses are true. Step 3: Since the conclusion is also true in that row, the argument is valid.
Captions
Feedback?

To use a truth table to establish the validity of an argument, a truth table is constructed for all the hypotheses and the conclusion. Each row in which all the hypotheses are true is examined. If the conclusion is true in each of the examined rows, then the argument is valid. If there is any row in which all the hypotheses are true but the conclusion is false, then the argument is invalid.

An argument can be shown to be invalid by showing an assignment of truth values to its variables that makes all the hypotheses true and the conclusion false. For example, the argument:



is invalid, because when and , the hypotheses and are both true, but the conclusion is false. It may be necessary to build the whole truth table in order to actually find a truth assignment that shows an argument is invalid. But the final proof of invalidity only requires a single truth assignment for which all the hypotheses are true and the conclusion is false.

The form of an argument

The hypotheses and conclusion in a logical argument can also be expressed in English, as in:

It is raining today.
If it is raining today, I will not ride my bike to school.
I will not ride my bike to school.

The form of an argument expressed in English is obtained by replacing each individual proposition with a variable. While it is common to express a logical argument in English, the validity of an argument is established by analyzing its form. Define propositional variables and to be:

It is raining today.
I will not ride my bike to school.

The argument's form, given below, was already shown to be valid by using a truth table:

Feedback?

When arguments are expressed in English, the propositions sometimes have known truth values. In the argument below, the hypotheses and conclusion are all true:
is not an even number.
If is an even number, then is an even number.
is not an even number.

The argument is invalid because its form

was shown to be invalid.

In a valid argument, the conclusion must follow from the hypotheses for every possible combination of truth values for the individual propositions. The invalid argument form shown above can be used with a different set of propositions to reach a false conclusion even when the hypotheses are true, as in:
is not an even number.
If is an even number, then is an even number.
is not an even number.

1.12 Rules of inference with propositions

Skip to main content
zybooks

    My library >
    ICS 253: Discrete Structures Fall 26 home >
    1.12: Rules of inference with propositions

1.11 Logical reasoning
1.12 Rules of inference with propositions
Learn to:

    Identify the rules of inference known to be valid arguments.
    Translate an argument expressed in English into logic symbols.
    Determine truth values that provide a counterexample for an invalid argument.
    Construct a logical proof showing that a logical argument is valid using the rules of inference and laws of propositional logic.

Using truth tables to establish the validity of an argument can become tedious, especially if an argument uses many variables. The table below shows some useful rules, which are arguments that have already been shown to be valid. The rules in the table can then be used to show that other arguments are valid. The laws of propositional logic can also be used in establishing the validity of an argument.
Table 1.12.1: Some rules of inference known to be valid arguments.
Rule of inference 	Name

	Modus ponens

	Modus tollens
	Addition
	Simplification

	Conjunction

	Hypothetical syllogism

	Disjunctive syllogism

	Resolution
Feedback?

The validity of an argument can be established by applying the rules of inference and laws of propositional logic in a logical proof. A logical proof of an argument is a sequence of steps, each consisting of a proposition and a justification. If the proposition in a step is a hypothesis, the justification is "Hypothesis." Otherwise, the proposition must follow from previous steps by applying one law of logic or rule of inference. The justification indicates which rule or law is used and the previous steps to which it is applied. For example, having justification "modus ponens, , " on line means that the modus ponens rule is applied to the propositions on lines and , resulting in the proposition on line The proposition in the last step in the proof must be the conclusion of the argument being proven. For example, in the animation below, the last proposition "" in step is the same as the conclusion of the argument being proven.
participation activity
1.12.1: An example of a logical proof to establish the validity of an argument.
Start
Static figure: Argument: \(\begin{align} & (p \vee r) \rightarrow q\\ &q \rightarrow t\\ \hline &\therefore t \end{align}\) Proof:
\(1.\) 	\(r\) 	Hypothesis
\(2.\) 	\(p \vee r\) 	Addition, \(1\)
\(3.\) 	\((p \vee r) \rightarrow q\) 	Hypothesis
\(4.\) 	\( q\) 	Modus ponens, \(2\), \(3\)
\(5.\) 	\(q \rightarrow t\) 	Hypothesis
\(6.\) 	\(t\) 	Modus ponens, \(4\), \(5\)
Step 1: An argument has hypotheses \((p \vee r) \to q, q \to t\), and \(r\). The conclusion is \(t\). The first line of the proof is \(r\), a hypothesis. Step 2: Line \(2\) of the proof is \(p ∨ r\), by Addition applied to line \(1.\) Step 3: Line \(3\) of the proof is \((p \vee r) \to q\), a hypothesis. Step 4: Line \(4\) of the proof is \(q\), by Modus Ponens applied to lines \(2\)and \(3.\) Step 5: Line \(5\) of the proof is \(q \to t\), a hypothesis. Step 6: Line \(6\) of the proof is \(t\), by Modus Ponens applied to lines \(4\) and \(5\), which is the conclusion of the argument.
Captions
Feedback?

Here is an argument expressed in English:
If it is raining or windy or both, the game will be cancelled.
The game will not be cancelled.
It is not windy.

The first step in proving the validity of the argument is to assign variable names to each of the individual propositions:

    It is windy
    It is raining
    The game will be cancelled

Replacing English phrases with variable names results in the following argument form:


The final step is to prove that the argument is valid using a logical proof.
Table 1.12.2: An example of a logical proof to establish the validity of an argument.
		Hypothesis

		Commutative law,

A note on using laws of propositions in logical reasoning proofs

The laws of propositional logic establish that certain pairs of propositions are logically equivalent. For example, De Morgan's law says that . Therefore, implies . Also, implies .

By contrast, proofs of logical arguments have a well defined direction. For example, the proof above shows that if and are both true, then is also true. But the conclusion does not necessarily imply that the two hypotheses are true.

It's logically correct to use the laws of propositions in proving that a logical argument is valid since only one direction of the equivalence is required. For example, in going from Step to Step in the above proof, one uses the fact that implies which follows from the fact that .

The laws of propositional logic can be applied in logical reasoning proofs to compound expressions, as in .

The laws of propositional logic can also be applied in logical reasoning proofs within compound expressions, as in .

It rained.

1.12.5: Proving arguments in English are valid or invalid.

(a)

If I get a job, then I will buy a new car and a new house.
I won't buy a new house.
I will not get a job.

Solution
(b)

If I get a job, then I will buy a new car or a new house (or both).
I won't buy a new house.
I will not get a job.

Solution
(c)

I will buy a new car and a new house only if I get a job.
I am not going to get a job.
I will not buy a new car.

Solution
(d)

I will buy a new car and a new house only if I get a job.
I am not going to get a job.
I will buy a new house.
I will not buy a new car.

1.13 Rules of inference with quantifiers

Skip to main content
zybooks

    ICS 253: Discrete Structures Fall 26 home >
    1.13: Rules of inference with quantifiers

1.12 Rules of inference with propositions
1.13 Rules of inference with quantifiers
Learn to:

    Distinguish between arbitrary and particular elements in a domain.
    Translate an argument with quantifiers expressed in English to logical symbols.
    Construct a logical proof showing that an argument with quantified statements is valid using the rules of inference for quantified statements.
    Show an argument with quantified statements is invalid by defining a domain and predicates for a counterexample.

To apply the rules of inference to quantified expressions, such as , the quantifier must be removed by plugging in a value from the domain to replace the variable A value that can be plugged in for variable is called an element of the domain of For example, if the domain is the set of all integers, is a proposition to which De Morgan's law can be applied. Elements of the domain can be defined in a hypothesis of an argument. In the argument below, the domain is a set of employees at a company. The second line defines as an element of the domain.

Every employee who received a large bonus works hard.
is an employee at the company.
received a large bonus.
Some employee works hard.

Elements of the domain can also be introduced within a proof with generic names such as "" or "" There are two types of named elements used in logical proofs. An arbitrary element of a domain has no special properties other than those shared by all the elements of the domain. A particular element of the domain may have properties that are not shared by all the elements of the domain. For example, if the domain is the set of all integers, is a particular element of the domain. The number is odd, which is not a property that is shared by all integers.

Every domain element referenced in a proof must be defined on a separate line of the proof. If the element is defined in a hypothesis, it is always a particular element and the definition of that element in the proof is labeled "Hypothesis." If an element is introduced for the first time in the proof, the definition is labeled "Element definition" and must specify whether the element is arbitrary or particular. In this material, an arbitrary element can only be introduced if the domain is non-empty. This restriction is adopted to avoid unsound proofs which can arise with arbitrary elements from empty domains. Throughout this material, the domains are assumed to be non-empty.
participation activity
1.13.1: Definitions of arbitrary and particular elements of a domain.
For each definition of an element in the domain, indicate whether the element defined is particular or arbitrary.
1)

The domain is the set of all integers.

is an integer. Hypothesis.
2)

The domain is the set of all employees at a company.

is an arbitrary employee of the company. Element definition.
3)

The domain is the set of all integers.

is a particular integer. Element definition.
4)

The domain is the set of students enrolled for a class.

is enrolled in the class. Hypothesis.
Feedback?

The rules existential instantiation and universal instantiation replace a quantified variable with an element of the domain. The rules existential generalization and universal generalization replace an element of the domain with a quantified variable. Note that the rules only apply to non-nested quantifiers. Applying the rules of inference to nested quantifiers requires more constraints on which rules can be applied in particular situations.
Table 1.13.1: Rules of inference for quantified statements.
Rule of Inference 	Name 	Example
is an element (arbitrary or particular)

	Universal instantiation 	is a student in the class.
Every student in the class completed the assignment.
Therefore, completed the assignment.
is an arbitrary element

	Universal generalization 	Let be an arbitrary integer.

Therefore, every integer is less than or equal to its square.

is a particular element 	Existential instantiation* 	There is an integer that is equal to its square.
Therefore, , for some integer
is an element (arbitrary or particular)

	Existential generalization 	is a particular student in the class.
completed the assignment.
Therefore, there is a student in the class who completed the assignment.

*Each use of existential instantiation must define a new element with its own name (Ex: "" or "").

Note: In existential instantiation, the variable definition is part of the conclusion in the rule because the application of existential instantiation is tied to that particular variable. But when applying universal instantiation, any variable defined earlier in the proof can be plugged in.

1.13.2: A logical proof that uses the rules of inference for quantified statements.
Start
Static figure: Argument: \(\begin{align} &\forall x (P(x) \vee Q(x)) \\ &3 \text{ is an integer} \\ &\neg P(3) \\ \hline &\therefore Q(3) \end{align}\) Proof:
\(1.\) 	\(\forall x (P(x) \vee Q(x))\) 	Hypothesis
\(2.\) 	\(3\) is an integer 	Hypothesis
\(3.\) 	\(P(3) \vee Q(3)\) 	Universal instantiation, \(1\), \(2\)
\(4.\) 	\(\neg P(3)\) 	Hypothesis
\(5.\) 	\(Q(3)\) 	Disjunctive syllogism, \(3\), \(4\)
Step 1: An argument has hypotheses \(\forall x (P(x) ∨ Q(x))\), "\(3\) is an integer," and \(\neg P(3)\). The conclusion is \(Q(3)\). The first two lines in the proof are the first two hypotheses. Step 2: Line \(3\)of the proof is \((P(3) ∨ Q(3))\), by universal instantiation applied to lines \(1\) and \(2\). Step 3: Line \(4\) of the proof is \(\neg P(3)\), a hypothesis. Step 4: Line \(5\)of the proof is \(Q(3)\), by disjunctive syllogism applied to lines \(3\)and \(4.\)

Multiple uses of existential instantiation: a common mistake

A new particular element must be defined with a new name for each use of existential instantiation within the same logical proof in order to avoid a faulty proof that an invalid argument is valid.

The mistake in the proof below is in the assumption that the value (introduced in Step ), which causes to be true, is the same value (introduced in Step ) that causes to be true. A correct use of existential instantiation in line would first introduce a new particular element, that is not necessarily equal to , and assert that is true. For example, if means that owns a cat and means that owns a dog, then the two hypotheses say that there is someone who owns a cat and there is someone who owns a dog. But the two hypotheses together do not imply that there is someone who owns a cat and a dog.
Table 1.13.2: Incorrect use of existential instantiation leading to an erroneous proof of an invalid argument.

1.14 Additional Exercises in Propositional Logic
1.) What is the negation of "Linda is younger than Sanjay"?

Answer: Linda is older than or the same age as Sanjay.

Another answer: It is not the case that Linda is younger than Sanjay.


2.) What is the negation of "Mei makes more money than Isabella"?

Answer: Mei makes less money than or the same as Isabella.

Another answer: It is not the case that Mei makes more money than Isabella.


3.) What is the negation of "Moshe is taller than Monica."?

Answer: Moshe is shorter than or the same height as Monica.

Another answer: It is not the case that Moshe is taller than Monica..


4.) What is the negation of "Abby is richer than Ricardo."?

Answer: Abby is poorer than or has the same wealth as Ricardo.

Another answer: It is not the case that Abby is richer than Ricardo..


5.) Let and be the propositions “Swimming at the New Jersey shore is allowed” and “Sharks have been spotted near the shore,” respectively. Express each of these compound propositions as an English sentence.

a)  

Answer: Sharks have not been spotted near the shore.

b)

Answer: Swimming at the New Jersey shore is allowed and sharks have been spotted near the shore.

c)

Answer: Swimming at the New Jersey shore is not allowed, or sharks have been spotted near the shore.

d)

Answer: If swimming at the New Jersey shore is allowed, then sharks have not been spotted near the shore.

e) 

Answer: If sharks have not been spotted near the shore, then swimming at the New Jersey shore is allowed.

f)

Answer: If swimming at the New Jersey shore is not allowed, then sharks have not been spotted near the shore.

g) 

Answer: Swimming at the New Jersey shore is allowed if and only if sharks have not been spotted near the shore.

h) 

Answer: Swimming at the New Jersey shore is not allowed, and either swimming is allowed or sharks have not been spotted near the shore.


6.)  Let and be the propositions : “It is below freezing.” : “It is snowing.” Task. Write the statements using , , and logical connectives (with negations).

a) It is below freezing and snowing.

Answer: 

b) It is below freezing but not snowing.

Answer: 

c) It is not below freezing and it is not snowing.

Answer: 

d) It is either snowing or below freezing (or both).

Answer:

e) If it is below freezing, it is also snowing.

Answer: 

f) Either it is below freezing or it is snowing, but it is not snowing if it is below freezing.

Answer: 

g) That it is below freezing is necessary and sufficient for it to be snowing.

Answer: 


7.) Let , , and be the propositions : “Grizzly bears have been seen in the area.” : “Hiking is safe on the trail.” : “Berries are ripe along the trail.”

Task. Write these propositions using , , and and logical connectives (including negations).

a) Berries are ripe along the trail, but grizzly bears have not been seen in the area.

Answer:

b) Grizzly bears have not been seen in the area and hiking on the trail is safe, but berries are ripe along the trail.

Answer:

c) If berries are ripe along the trail, hiking is safe if and only if grizzly bears have not been seen in the area.

Answer:

d) Itis not safe to hike on the trail, but grizzly bears have not been seen in the area and the berries along the trail are ripe.

Answer:

e) For hiking on the trail to be safe, it is necessary but not sufficient that berries not be ripe along the trail and for grizzly bears not to have been seen in the area.

Answer:


8.) State the converse, contrapositive, and inverse of each of these conditional statements.

“If it snows today, I will ski tomorrow.”
