4.1.2: Recognizing well-defined functions.
Start
Static figure: Text: \(f:X\to A\) Two columns are shown with elements in the domain lined vertically in the left column and elements in the target in the right column. Step 1: In an arrow diagram for \(f\), each element in the domain has exactly one arrow leaving it. The domain elements are in set \(X\). \(X = \{w, x, y, z \}\). The target elements are in set \(A\). \(A = \{ a, b, c, d \}\). The function is \(f = \{( w, a ),( x, a ),( y, d ,)( z, c ) \}\). An arrow goes from \(w\) to \(a\), \(x\) to \(a\), \(y\) to \(d\), and \(z\) to \(c\). Step 2: The range of \(f\) is the set \(\{a, c, d\}\) because \(a\), \(c\), and \(d\) are the target elements with at least one arrow coming in. In the right column the elements \(a\), \(c\), \(d\) are circled. Step 3: If the arrow from \(x\) to \(a\) is removed, \(f\) is no longer a function because \(f\) is not defined on input \(x\). The ordered pair \((x, a)\) in \(f\) has a large x placed over it, and the arrow going from \(x\) to \(a\) is faded. Text: \(f\) is no longer a function because \(f\) is not defined on \(x\). Step 4: If an arrow from \(y\) to \(b\) is added, \(f\) is no longer a function because \(f\) maps \(y\) to both \(d\) and \(b\). A new ordered pair \((y, b)\) is added to the original set \(f\). An arrow appears from \(y\) to \(b\). \(y\) now has two arrows starting at it. One goes to \(d\) and the other goes to \(b\). Text: \(f\) is no longer a function because \((y, b), (y, d) \in f\)

4.2 Floor and ceiling functions

Skip to main content
zybooks

    My library >
    ICS 253: Discrete Structures Fall 26 home >
    4.2: Floor and ceiling functions

4.1 Definition of functions
4.2 Floor and ceiling functions
Learn to:

    Evaluate the floor and ceiling functions of a real number.
    Compute the value of an expression with nested floor and ceiling functions.
    Formulate a mathematical expression using the floor and ceiling functions to solve an application problem.

The floor function and the ceiling function map real numbers onto integers. The floor and ceiling functions round real numbers to a nearby integer in different ways.
The floor function

The floor function maps a real number to the nearest integer in the downward direction.
The floor function comes up so often in mathematics that it has its own notation:
For example, and The floor function also rounds negative numbers to the nearest integer in the downward direction: and
The ceiling function

The ceiling function rounds a real number to the nearest integer in the upward direction.
Like the floor function, the ceiling function also has its own notation:
For example, and The ceiling function also rounds negative numbers to the nearest integer in the upward direction: and
participation activity
4.2.1: Computing the floor and ceiling functions.
Start
Static figure: Six number lines are shown with tick marks going from \(-2\) to \(2\). Step 1: The floor function is computed by sliding down to the nearest integer. The floor of \(1.8\) is \(1\). A number line appears with an arrow pointing to \(1.8\). An arrow labeled \(\lfloor 1.8 \rfloor\) starts at the point \(1.8\) then slides down to the integer \(1\) on the number line. Step 2: The floor of \(-1.3\) is \(-2\). A number line appears with an arrow pointing to \(-1.3\). An arrow labeled \(\lfloor -1.3 \rfloor\) starts at the point \(-1.3\) then slides down to the integer \(-2\) on the number line. Step 3: The ceiling function is computed by sliding up to the nearest integer. The ceiling of \(0.3\) is \(1\). A number line appears with an arrow pointing to \(0.3\). An arrow labeled \(\lceil 1.8 \rceil\) starts at the point \(0.3\) then slides up to the integer \(1\) on the number line. Step 4: The ceiling of \(-0.8\) is \(0\). A number line appears with an arrow pointing to \(-0.8\). An arrow labeled \(\lceil -.8 \rceil\) starts at the point \(-0.8\) then slides up to the integer \(0\) on the number line. Step 5: The ceiling and the floor of an integer are the same as the integer. The floor of \(-1\) is \(-1\). The ceiling of \(-1\) is \(-1\). A number line appears with an arrow pointing to \(-1\). The label \(-1\) on top of the arrow is overwritten with \(\lfloor -1 \rfloor\). Another number line appears with an arrow pointing to \(2\). The label \(2\) on top of the arrow is overwritten with \(\lceil 2 \rceil\).

4.3 Properties of functions

Skip to main content
zybooks

    My library >
    ICS 253: Discrete Structures Fall 26 home >
    4.3: Properties of functions

4.2 Floor and ceiling functions
4.3 Properties of functions
Learn to:

    Determine if a function is one-to-one, onto, both (bijective), or neither.
    Use an arrow diagram to identify function properties.
    Compare the relative sizes of the domain and target of a function to determine if it is possible that the function satisfies a certain property.
    Select a function that satisfies a function property.
    Provide a counterexample to show that a function does not have a specified function property.

A function is one-to-one or injective if implies that . That is, maps different elements in to different elements in .

A function is onto or surjective if the range of is equal to the target . That is, for every there is an such that .

The properties of being one-to-one or onto are important in many situations. For example, consider a function that maps employees to offices. If the function is one-to-one, then no one has to share an office. If the function is onto, then no offices are empty, and the company's space is well utilized. Functions are used to define an assignment of processes to computers in a distributed network. If the function is one-to-one, then no computer is time-sharing between different tasks. If the function is onto, then all the resources of the network are being used.

A function is bijective if it is both one-to-one and onto. A bijective function is called a bijection. A bijection is also called a one-to-one correspondence.
participation activity
4.3.1: One-to-one and onto functions.
Start
Static figure: Text: \(f:X\to A\) Two columns are shown with elements in the domain lined vertically in the left column and elements in the target in the right column. The domain elements are in set \(X\). \(X = \{w, x, y, z \}\). The target elements are in set \(A\). \(A = \{ a, b, c, d, e \}\). Step 1: \(f\) is not one-to-one because \(f(w) = f(z) = c\). An arrow diagram appears. An arrow goes from \(w\) to \(c\), \(x\) to \(b\), \(y\) to \(a\), and \(z\) to \(c\). In the target, the element \(c\) is circled. Step 2: \(f\) is not onto because no elements in \(X\) map to \(d\) or \(e\). In the target, the elements \(d\) and \(e\) are circled. Step 3: Now \(f\) is one-to-one but not onto. The function changes in this step. The arrow that had gone from \(w\) to \(c\) is now going from \(w\) to \(d\). The element \(e\) is circled to show it still does not have an element from the domain mapping to it. Step 4: Now \(f\) is one-to-one and onto. \(f\) is a bijection. Set \(A\) changes in this step, element \(e\) is removed from set \(A\).

When the domain and target are finite sets, information can be inferred about their relative sizes based on whether the function is one-to-one or onto.

    If is onto, then for every element in the target, there is at least one element in the domain: .
    If is one-to-one, then every element in the domain maps to a unique element in the target: .
    If is a bijection, then is one-to-one and onto: and , which implies that .

Figure 4.3.1: Relative sizes of the domain and target from function properties.
Three arrow diagrams. In each diagram, there are two columns with elements in the domain lined vertically in the left column and elements in the target in the right column. The first diagram is labeled: onto |D| ≥ |T|, arrows go from a to x, b to w, c to y, d to w, and e to z. The second diagram is labeled: one-to-one |D| ≤ |T|, arrows go from a to x, b to u, c to y, d to w, and no arrow goes to z in the range. The third diagram is labeled: bijection |D| ≤ |T| and |D| ≥ |T|, |D| = |T|, arrows go from a to u, b to x, c to y, and d to w.
Feedback?

The fact that the domain and target of a bijection have the same size may seem simple, but this fact is extremely powerful. One way to count the elements in a set is to define a bijection between that set and another set whose size is already known. Counting the elements in a set is a fundamental part of discrete probability, an important tool in many areas of science.

4.4 The inverse of a function

Skip to main content
zybooks

    My library >
    ICS 253: Discrete Structures Fall 26 home >
    4.4: The inverse of a function

4.3 Properties of functions
4.4 The inverse of a function
Learn to:

    Determine if a function has a well-defined inverse.
    Calculate the inverse of a function at a specific value.
    Describe the input/output relationship of the inverse of a function.
    Solve for the inverse of a function with an infinite domain analytically.

If a function is a bijection, then the inverse of is obtained by exchanging the first and second entries in each pair in . The inverse of is denoted by :

Reversing each pair in a function does not always result in a well-defined function. Therefore, some functions do not have an inverse. A function has an inverse if and only if reversing each pair in results in a well-defined function from to . is a well-defined function if every element in is mapped to exactly one element in .
participation activity
4.4.1: Arrow diagram for the inverse of a function.
Start
Static figure: Set \(X\) and set \(Y\) are defined. \(X = \{ 1, 2, 3 \}\). \(Y = \{ 7, 8, 9\}\). Four arrow diagrams are shown. Step 1: The arrows in an arrow diagram for function \(f\) are reversed to determine if \(f\) has an inverse. An arrow diagram appears with the elements of set \(X\) lined vertically in the left column and the elements of set \(Y\) in the right column. This diagram is labeled \(f: X\to Y\). The function is \(f = \{( 1, 7 ),( 2, 9 ),( 3, 9 ) \}\). Arrows in the diagram go from \(1\) to \(7\), \(2\) to \(9\), and \(3\) to \(9\). A second arrow diagram appears with the elements of set \(Y\) lined vertically in the left column and the elements of set \(X\) in the right column. This diagram is labeled \(f^{-1} = \{ (7, 1), (9, 2), (9, 3) \}\). Arrows in the diagram go from \(7\) to \(1\), \(9\) to \(2\), and \(9\) to \(3\). Step 2: If arrows for \(f\) are reversed, the result is not a function because 8 has no outgoing arrow, and also \(9\) has two outgoing arrows. Thus, \(f\) does not have an inverse. It is stated \(f^{-1}\) is not a function. Step 3: The arrows in an arrow diagram for function \(g\) are reversed to determine if \(f\) has an inverse. An arrow diagram appears with the elements of set \(X\) lined vertically in the left column and the elements of set \(Y\) in the right column. This diagram is labeled \(g: X\to Y\). The function is \(g = \{( 1, 9 ),( 2, 7 ),( 3, 8 ) \}\). Arrows in the diagram go from \(1\) to \(9\), \(2\) to \(7\), and \(3\) to \(8\). A second arrow diagram appears with the elements of set \(Y\) lined vertically in the left column and the elements of set \(X\) in the right column. This diagram is labeled \(g^{-1}: Y\to X\) and \(g^{-1} = \{ (7, 2), (8, 3), (9, 1) \}\). Arrows in the diagram go from \(7\) to \(2\), \(8\) to \(3\), and \(9\) to \(1\). Step 4: If the arrows for \(g\) are reversed, the result is a function because each left element has exactly one outgoing arrow. Thus, \(g\) has an inverse. It is stated \(g^{-1}\) is a function. \(g\) has an inverse defined by \(g^{-1}(7) = 2\), \(g^{-1}(8) = 3\), \(g^{-1}(9) = 1\).
Captions
Feedback?

The finite examples in the animation above show that is obtained by reversing the arrows in the arrow diagram for . The resulting is a function, if and only if every element in has exactly one outgoing arrow after the arrows are reversed, which in turn holds if and only if is a bijection.

The reasoning above also applies to functions with infinite domains. In summary:

A function has an inverse if and only if is a bijection.

Recall that a bijection is a function that is one-to-one and onto.

4.4.4: Solving for the inverse of a function analytically.
Start
Static figure: An analytical process to solve for the inverse of a function is displayed. Step 1: To check that f is one-to-one, check that \(x \neq x\prime\), implies \(3x - 2 \neq 3x\prime - 2\). Equivalently, show the contrapositive: \(3x - 2 = 3x\prime - 2\), implies \(x = x\prime\). Text at the top of these two statements is \(f: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}\) \(f(x) = 3x - 2\) Step 2: \(f\) is one-to-one because if \(3x - 2 = 3x\prime - 2\), then \(x = x\prime\). The following algebraic explanation appears: \(3x - 2 = 3\prime - 2\). Add \(2\) to both sides. \(3x = 3x\prime\). Divide by \(3\). \(\frac{x}{3} = \frac{x\prime}{3}\) \(x = x\prime\). \(f\) is one to one. Step 3: \(f\) is onto because for every \(y\), there is an \(x\) such that \(3x - 2 = y\). Text appears: Check that \(f\) is onto: for every real number \(y\), there is an \(x\) such that \(f(x) = 3x - 2 = y\). Step 4: The inverse of \(f\) can be found by solving for an expression that gives the value of \(x\) in terms of \(y\). The following algebraic explanation appears: Solve for \(x\) in terms of \(y\). \(3x - 2 = y\). Add \(2\) to both sides. \(3x = y +2\). Divide by 3. \(\frac{3x}{3} = \frac{(y+2)}{3}\). \(x = \frac{(y+2)}{3}\) Step 5: The inverse of \(f\) on input \(y\) is \(\frac{(y + 2)}{3}\). Text appears: This is \(f^{-1}\) \(f^{-1}: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}\) \(f^{-1}(y) = \frac{(y+2)}{3}\)
Captions

Example 4.4.1: Encrypting messages.

The process of encrypting messages can be expressed through the mathematical language of functions. Consider encrypting -digit credit card numbers. Let be the set of all possible -digit numbers. An encryption scheme for is a function . To send a credit card number over an insecure communication channel, the sender encrypts by computing . The encryption function is chosen to be a bijection. The sender then sends over the channel. The receiver receives and wishes to decrypt the message to obtain the original number . To accomplish this, the receiver applies the inverse function to in order to get the credit card number . The sender and receiver must communicate ahead of time so that the receiver knows what function to apply to the number that is received. A secure encryption scheme must have the property that without foreknowledge of the function , it would be difficult to determine from .

4.4 The inverse of a function
4.5 Composition of functions
Learn to:

    Calculate the composition of two or more functions at a specific value.
    Determine the domain, target, and range of the composition of two or more functions.
    Understand the relationship between a bijection, its inverse, and the identity function.
    Analytically find a mathematical expression for the composition of functions.

Let be a function that assigns employees to offices in a company. Let be the function that maps each office to the telephone number for the phone in that office. An employee, Rajiv, is assigned the office . Rajiv's work phone number is . The process of applying a function to the result of another function is called composition.
Definition 4.5.1: Composition of functions.
and are two functions, where and . The composition of with , denoted is the function such that for all .
Feedback?
participation activity
4.5.1: Composition of functions.
Start
Static figure: Three arrow diagrams are shown. Three sets are defined: \(X = \{1, 2, 3 \}\) \(Y = \{a, b, c \}\) \(Z = \{7, 10, 12 \}\) Step 1: Function \(f\) has domain \(X\) and target \(Y\). \(g\) has domain \(Y\) and target \(Z\). Therefore, the composition of \(g\) with \(f\) is a function with domain \(X\) and target \(Z\). The first arrow diagram appears with the elements of set \(X\) lined vertically in the left column and the elements of set \(Y\) in the right column. This diagram is labeled \(f: X\to Y\). Arrows in the diagram go from \(1\) to \(a\), \(2\) to \(a\), and \(3\) to \(b\). A second arrow diagram appears with the elements of set \(Y\) lined vertically in the left column and the elements of set \(Z\) in the right column. This diagram is labeled \(f: Y\to Z\). Arrows in the diagram go from \(a\) to \(10\), \(b\) to \(10\), and \(c\) to \(12\). A third arrow diagram appears with the elements of set \(X\) lined vertically in the left column and the elements of set \(Z\) in the right column. This diagram is labeled \(g \circ f: X\to Z\). Step 2: \(f\) maps \(1\) to \(a\) and \(g\) maps \(a\) to \(10\). Therefore, \(g\) composed with \(f\) maps \(1\) to \(10\). The arrow going from \(1\) to \(a\) in function \(f\)'s arrow diagram is highlighted. The arrow going from \(a\) to \(10\) in function \(g\)'s diagram is highlighted. An arrow now appears from \(1\) to \(10\) in the composition's diagram. Step 3: Since \(f(2) = a\) and \(g(a) = 10\), then \(( g \circ f )(2) = 10\). Also, since \(f(3) = b\) and \(g(b) = 10\), then \(( g \circ f )(3) = 10\). In the composition's diagram, an arrow appears from \(2\) to \(10\) and another from \(3\) to \(10\).

4.6 Cardinality of Sets
Introduction

The cardinality of a finite set is the number of elements in the set. We use the cardinalities of finite sets to tell us when they have the same size, or when one is bigger than the other. In this section, we extend this notion to infinite sets. That is, we will define what it means for two infinite sets to have the same cardinality, providing us with a way to measure the relative sizes of infinite sets. We will be particularly interested in countably infinite sets, which are sets with the same cardinality as the set of positive integers. We will establish the surprising result that the set of rational numbers is countably infinite. We will also provide an example of an uncountable set when we show that the set of real numbers is not countable. The concepts developed in this section have important applications to computer science. A function is called uncomputable if no computer program can be written to find all its values, even with unlimited time and memory. We will use the concepts in this section to explain why uncomputable functions exist.


Definition 1: The sets and have the same cardinality if and only if there is a one-to-one correspondence from to . When and have the same cardinality, we write .

For infinite sets, the definition of cardinality provides a relative measure of the sizes of two sets, rather than a measure of the size of one particular set. We can also define what it means for one set to have a smaller cardinality than another set.

Definition 2: If there is a one-to-one function from to , the cardinality of is less than or the same as the cardinality of , and we write . Moreover, when and and have different cardinality, we say that the cardinality of is less than the cardinality of and we write .

Remark: In Definitions 1 and 2, we introduced the notations and to denote that and have the same cardinality and that the cardinality of is less than the cardinality of . However, these definitions do not give any separate meaning to and when and are arbitrary infinite sets.
Countable Sets

We will now split infinite sets into two groups: those with the same cardinality as the set of natural numbers and those with a different cardinality.

Definition 3: A set that is either finite or has the same cardinality as the set of positive integers is called countable. A set that is not countable is called uncountable. When an infinite set is countable, we denote the cardinality of by (where is aleph, the first letter of the Hebrew alphabet). We write and say that S has cardinality "aleph null."
Example 1: 

Show that the set of odd positive integers is a countable set.
Solution:

To show that the set of odd positive integers is countable, we will exhibit a one-to-one correspondence between this set and the set of positive integers. Consider the function form to the set of odd positive integers. We show that f is a one-to-one correspondence by showing that it is both one-to-one and onto. To see that it is one-to-one, suppose that . Then , so . To see that it is onto, suppose that t is an odd positive integer. Then is 1 less than an even integer , where k is a natural number. Hence . We display this one-to-one correspondence in the following figure.
Figure 4.6.1: A One-to-One Correspondence Between and the Set of Odd Positive Integers

An infinite set is countable if and only if it is possible to list the elements of the set in a sequence (indexed by the positive integers). The reason for this is that a one-to-one correspondence from the set of positive integers to a set S can be expressed in terms of a sequence , where .
HILBERT’S GRAND HOTEL

We now describe a paradox that shows that something impossible with finite sets may be possible with infinite sets. The famous mathematician David Hilbert invented the notion of the Grand Hotel, which has a countably infinite number of rooms, each occupied by a guest. When a new guest arrives at a hotel with a finite number of rooms, and all rooms are occupied, this guest cannot be accommodated without evicting a current guest. However, we can always accommodate a new guest at the Grand Hotel, even when all rooms are already occupied, as shown in the next example.
Example 2:

How can we accommodate a new guest arriving at the fully occupied Grand Hotel without removing any of the current guests?
Solution:

Because the rooms of the Grand Hotel are countable, we can list them as Room 1, Room 2, Room 3, and so on. When a new guest arrives, we move the guest in Room 1 to Room 2, the guest in Room 2 to Room 3, and in general, the guest in Room to Room , for all positive integers . This frees up Room 1, which we assign to the new guest, and all the current guests still have rooms. This is illustrated in the next figure.
Figure 4.6.2: A new guest arrives at Hilbert’s Grand Hotel.

When there are finitely many rooms in a hotel, the notion that all rooms are occupied is equivalent to the notion that no new guests can be accommodated. However, Hilbert’s paradox of the Grand Hotel can be explained by noting that this equivalence no longer holds when there are infinitely many rooms.
Examples of Countable and Uncountable Sets
Example 3:

Show that the set of all integers is countable.
Solution:

We can list all integers in a sequence by starting with and alternating between positive and negative integers: . Alternatively, we could find a one-to-one correspondence between the set of positive integers and the set of all integers. We leave it to the reader to show that the function when n is even and when n is odd is such a function. Consequently, the set of all integers is countable.

It is not surprising that the set of odd integers and the set of all integers are both countable sets. Many people are amazed to learn that the set of rational numbers is countable, as we will see in the next example.
Example 4:

Show that the set of positive rational numbers is countable.
Solution:

It may seem surprising that the set of positive rational numbers is countable, but we will show how we can list the positive rational numbers as a sequence . First, note that every positive rational number is the quotient of two positive integers.

We can arrange the positive rational numbers by listing those with denominator in the first row, those with denominator in the second row, and so on, as displayed in the figure below.

The key to listing the rational numbers in a sequence is to first list the positive rational numbers with , followed by those with , followed by those with , and so on, following the path shown in the figure below. Whenever we encounter a number that is already listed, we do not list it again. For example, when we come to , we do not list it because we have already listed . The initial terms in the list of positive rational numbers we have constructed are , and so on. These numbers are shown circled; the uncircled numbers in the list are those we leave out because they are already listed. Because all positive rational numbers are listed once, as the reader can verify, we have shown that the set of positive rational numbers is countable.
Figure 4.6.3: The positive rational numbers are countable.
An Uncountable Set

We have seen that the set of positive rational numbers is a countable set. Do we have a promising candidate for an uncountable set? The first place we might look is the set of real numbers. The following example uses the Cantor diagonalization argument to prove that the set of real numbers is not countable.
Example 5: 

Show that the set of real numbers is an uncountable set.
Solution:

To show that the set of real numbers is uncountable, we suppose that the set of real numbers is countable and arrive at a contradiction. Then, the subset of all real numbers that fall between and would also be countable (the reader can quickly prove that any subset of a countable set is also countable). Under this assumption, the real numbers between and can be listed in some order, say, . Let the decimal representation of these real numbers be

where . (For example, if , we have , and so on.) Then, form a new real number with decimal expansion , where the decimal digits are determined by the following rule:

(As an example, suppose that , and so on. Then we have , where because , because , because , because , and so on.)

Every real number has a unique decimal expansion (when the possibility that the expansion has a tail end that consists entirely of the digit is excluded). Therefore, the real number is not equal to any of because the decimal expansion of differs from the decimal expansion of in the th place to the right of the decimal point, for each . Because there is a real number between and that is not in the list, the assumption that all the real numbers between and could be listed must be false. Therefore, all the real numbers between and cannot be listed, so the set of real numbers between and is uncountable. Any set with an uncountable subset is uncountable (the reader should prove this). Hence, the set of real numbers is uncountable.

5.1 The Division Algorithm
Learn to:

    Use notation and terminology associated with divisibility for integers.
    Determine properties of integer divisibility of linear combinations of numbers.
    Determine the divisors of a given number.
    Apply the Division Algorithm to compute quotients and remainders when dividing integers.
    Justify and prove statements related to divisibility and remainders.

The first mathematical objects most people encounter are integers. Integers are a part of everyday life and easy to understand. Even so, some of the deepest mathematical mysteries are found in number theory, the branch of mathematics concerned with the study of integers. Number theory was studied primarily for its own sake for the better part of the last several thousand years, without any particular application as the goal. However, in the last few decades, number theory has emerged as a critical component of many applications, especially in computer science. In particular, number theory forms the mathematical basis for modern cryptography, the study of secure communication.

In integer division, the input and output values must always be integers. For example, when the number is divided by the answer is with a remainder of instead of . In this chapter, the term "number" refers to an integer as opposed to a real number.
Definition 5.1.1: Divides.
Let and be two integers. Then divides (denoted ) if and only if and there is an integer such that " does not divide " is denoted by If divides , then is said to be a multiple of , and is a factor or divisor of .

However, for the material on the properties of integers, the term "linear combination" assumes integer coefficients. A statement about a generic linear combination uses variables to denote the integer coefficients. For example, is a linear combination of and

Quotients and remainders

If does not divide , then dividing into results in a nonzero remainder. The following theorem, called the Division Algorithm, states that the result of the division (the quotient) and the remainder are unique. The Division Algorithm, as stated below, is not an algorithm at all but a statement of a mathematical fact. A procedural version that shows how to compute the quotient and the remainder is given later.
Theorem 5.1.2: The Division Algorithm.
Let be an integer and let be a positive integer. Then, there are unique integers and , with such that

5.2 Greatest common divisor and Euclid's algorithm

Skip to main content
zybooks

    My library >
    ICS 253: Discrete Structures Fall 26 home >
    5.2: Greatest common divisor and Euclid's algorithm

5.1 The Division Algorithm
5.2 Greatest common divisor and Euclid's algorithm
Learn to:

    Find the of two integers using Euclid's algorithm.
    Use Euclid's extended algorithm to express the of two numbers as a linear combination of those numbers.
    Find the multiplicative inverse of an integer by inspection.
    Find the multiplicative inverse of an integer using Euclid's extended algorithm.

The greatest common divisor of two numbers can be computed if the prime factorizations for the two numbers are already known. Unfortunately, computing the prime factorization for an integer is a hard problem. Cryptographic protocols require computing the of two numbers with hundreds of digits, and no way is known to compute the prime factorization of such large numbers in a reasonable amount of time. However, there is an efficient way to compute the of two numbers without finding their prime factorizations. The algorithm presented here is widely used today and is attributed to the Greek mathematician Euclid who lived around B.C. The basis of the algorithm is the following theorem:
Theorem 5.2.1: GCD Theorem.
Let and be two positive integers. Then

If an integer is selected, then is a function that takes a single integer as input and outputs , resulting in a number from the set For example, if , the operation maps to and to

Given the set , addition and multiplication are defined on the elements in this set in the usual way, except that the function is applied afterwards to ensure that the result is again from For example, adding and results in since Similarly, multiplying and results in since The operation defined by adding two numbers and applying to the result is called addition . The operation defined by multiplying two numbers and applying to the result is called multiplication .

The set with addition and multiplication defines a closed mathematical system with elements called a ring. The ring with addition and multiplication is denoted by . Many kinds of rings are studied in abstract mathematics. Application 5.3.1: Hash functions.

Consider a university database that stores important information about all enrolled students. Typically, a university issues each student an ID number with a fixed number of digits. If the number of digits in an ID number is , then the number of possible different ID numbers is million — clearly much larger than the number of students enrolled in the school.

The administration must keep each student's information easily accessible. In particular, the administration must be able to retrieve a student's records given the student's ID number or determine that the ID number does not match any of its currently enrolled students.

A common way to organize data is to maintain an array called a hash table that is slightly larger than the number of data items to be stored. A hash function is used to map each data item to a location in the array. The size of the student database would be larger than the number of students in the school but much smaller than the million possible IDs.

The goal is to devise a hash function that looks random (so that student records are scattered evenly around the hash table), but is deterministic to compute. That is, the location of a student's records must be reliably computed given the student's ID number.

Many kinds of hash functions are used in practice. The choice depends on the particular application. One common and efficiently computable hash function is where is the input ID number, is the size of the hash table and is a constant. Note that taking the result ensures that the output is in the range from through so that the output can be used to index directly into the hash table.
Feedback?
Application 5.3.2: Pseudo-random number generators.
Many computer applications use random numbers. However, truly random numbers are not easy to generate. As a substitute for random numbers, computers use functions called pseudo-random number generators that produce numbers having many of the statistical properties of random numbers but are deterministically generated. Devising good pseudo-random number generators is an ongoing research topic in computer science. Meanwhile, a variety of pseudo-random number generators are regularly used in practice.

One of the oldest and best known pseudo-random number generators is the linear congruential generator. The linear congruential generator starts with an initial value and generates each subsequent value as a function of the previous value according to the function The parameters , , and are chosen for efficiency and performance based on statistical tests. Each number generated lies in the range through and can be scaled if a different range is desired.
Feedback?

Precedence of the operation

In this material, the precedence of the operation is the same as multiplication and division, so the operation takes precedence over addition:
When the operation is applied to a product, the operations are performed left to right, so the multiplication operation is applied first:
Programming languages vary regarding the precedence of the operation. When using the operation in programming, one must be aware of the conventions used by the language. In this material, parentheses are often used to clarify the order in which operations should be performed.

In computing arithmetic expressions , taking intermediate results before the final result is obtained is sometimes convenient. 5.4 Fast exponentiation
Learn to:

    Use the binary expansion of the integer exponent to compute .
    Execute the iterative algorithm for fast exponentiation.
    Execute the iterative algorithm for fast modular exponentiation.

Suppose one wants to compute , for positive integers and Computing exponents is an important step in the RSA cryptosystem discussed later. One way to approach the computation is to start with some variable initialized to Then multiply times a total of times. This method for computing requires multiplication operations, which could take an impractical amount of time if is a large number. The below figure shows how it works.
Figure 5.4.1: Computing a power of by repeated multiplication by .
p = x. x squared is computed by p = p times x. x cubed is computed by p = p times x. x to a power of 4 is computed by p = p times x. x to a power of 5, and so on, is similarly computed by multiplying p times x.
Feedback?

One can also start with initialized to and keep squaring If the value of is currently for some integer and is squared, then its exponent goes up by a factor of : If the squaring is done repeatedly, then the exponent gets large faster, but the exponent is always a power of two, as shown below.
Figure 5.4.2: Computing a power of by repeated squaring.
p = x. x squared is computed by p = p times p. x to a power of 4 is computed by p = p times p. x to a power of 8 is computed by p = p times p. x to a power of 16, and so on, is similarly computed by multiplying p times p.
Feedback?

To compute to an arbitrary integer exponent , examine the binary expansion of : When two different powers of are multiplied, the exponents are added: Therefore, can be computed by multiplying different powers of in which the exponents are powers of : . Each coefficient is or If for some , then and the corresponding factor can be omitted from the product. The following animation illustrates all the ideas put together.
participation activity
5.4.1: An illustration of fast integer exponentiation.
Start
Static figure: An illustration of fast integer exponentiation is shown. Step 1: To compute \(7^{11}\), start with the binary expansion of \(11\), which is \((1011)_2.\) The place of each bit is numbered \(3\) to \(0\) from left to right. Step 2: Therefore, \(11\) can be expressed as \((1 \cdot 2^3) + (0\cdot 2^2) + (1 \cdot 2^1) + (1 \cdot 2^0) .\) Step 3: Therefore, \(7^{11} = 7^{(1 \cdot 2^3) + (0\cdot 2^2) + (1 \cdot 2^1) + (1 \cdot 2^0)}.\) Step 4: The expression is equal to \(7^{(1 \cdot 8)} \cdot 7^{(0\cdot 4)} \cdot 7^{(1 \cdot 2)} \cdot 7^{(1 \cdot 1)}.\) Step 5: The powers of \(7\) can be computed by repeated squaring: \(7^1 = 7\), \(7^2 = 49\), \(7^4 = 49^2 = 2401\), and \(7^8 = 2401^2 = 5764801.\) Step 6: Note that \(7^{(0 \cdot 4)} = 1\), so that factor can be dropped. \(7^{11} = 7^8 \cdot 7^2 \cdot 7^1 = 5764801 \cdot 49 \cdot 7 = 1977326743.\)

participation activity
5.4.3: A sample run of the iterative fast exponentiation algorithm.
Start
Static figure: A sample run of the iterative fast exponentiation algorithm is shown. The algorithm is on the right, and the present variable values for \(p, s, r\), and binary rep of \(r\) are displayed on the left. Begin Fast Exponentiation code: Input: \(x = 7\) \(y = 13\) Output: \(7^{13}\) \(p \colon= 1\) \(s \colon= x\) \(r \colon= y\) While (\(r > 0\)) If (\(r \text{ mod } 2 = 1)\) \( p \colon= p \cdot s\) \(s \colon= s \cdot s\) \(r \colon= r \text{ div } 2\) End-while Return (\(p\)) End Fast Exponentiation code. Step 1: FastExponentiation on input \(x = 7\) and \(y = 13\). Initialize variables to \(p = 1\), \(s = x\), and \(r = y\). The binary rep of \(y\) is \(1101.\) \(r = 13\). Step 2: \(r \text{ mod } 2\) is the last bit in the binary rep of \(r\). Since \(r \text{ mod } 2 = 1\), \(p\) is replaced with \(p \cdot s = 1 \cdot 7\). The value of \(s\) is squared and is now \(7^2\). In the algorithm, While (\(r \gt 0\)) is highlighted. If \((r ~\mbox{mod}~ 2 = 1)\) is highlighted next. An arrow labeled "last bit \(= r \text{ mod } 2\)" appears pointing to the last \(1\) in the binary rep of \(r. p \colon= p \cdot \)s is now highlighted. The variable \(p\) is updated to \(1 \times 7\). \(s \colon= s \cdot s\) is now highlighted. Variable \(s\) is updated to \((7^2)\). Step 3: \(r\) is replaced with \( r\text{ div } 2\), which is \(6.\) The binary rep of \(r\) changes by dropping the last bit. In the algorithm, \(r \colon= r \text{ div } 2\) is highlighted. \(r: 13 \text{ div } 2 = 6\) is displayed, updating \(r\) to \(6.\) Binary rep of \(r\) is updated to \(110\). In the algorithm, End-while is highlighted, and then While \((r \gt 0)\) is highlighted. Step 4: The last bit in the binary rep of \(r\) is \(0\), so \(p\) does not change. \(s\) is squared and has a new value of \(7^4\). In the algorithm, If \((r ~\mbox{mod}~ 2 = 1)\) is highlighted next. An arrow labeled "last bit \(= r \text{ mod } 2\)" appears pointing to the \(0\) in the binary rep of \(r\). \(s \colon= s \cdot s\) is now highlighted. Variable \(s\) is updated to \([(7^2)]^2 = 7^{2 \times 2} = 7^4\). Step 5: \(r\) is replaced by \(r \text{ div } 2\), so the new value of \(r\) is \(6 \text{ div } 2 = 3\). The new binary rep of \(r\) is obtained by dropping the last bit. In the algorithm,\( r \colon= r \text{ div } 2\) is highlighted. \(r\colon 6 \text{ div } 2 = 3\) is displayed, updating \(r\) to \(3.\) Binary rep of \(r\) is updated to \(11\). In the algorithm, End-while is highlighted, and then While \((r \gt 0)\) is highlighted. Step 6: In the next iteration,\( r \text{ mod } 2 = 1\), so \(p\) is multiplied by \(s\) and has a new value of \(7 \cdot 7^4\). \(s\) is squared and becomes \(7^8\). \(r\) becomes \((r \text{ div } 2)\) and has a new value of \(1.\) In the algorithm, If \((r ~\mbox{mod}~ 2 = 1)\) is highlighted next. An arrow labeled "last bit \(= r \text{ mod } 2\)" appears pointing to the last \(1\) in the binary rep of \(r\). \(p \colon= p \cdot s\) is now highlighted. The variable \(p\) is updated to \(1 \times 7 \times 7^4\). \(s \colon= s \cdot s\) is now highlighted. Variable \(s\) is updated to \([(7^4)]^2 = 7^{4 \times 2} = 7^8\). \(r \colon= r \text{ div } 2\) is now highlighted. \(r: 3 \text{ div } 2 = 1\) is displayed, updating \(r\) to \(1.\) Binary rep of \(r\) is updated to \(1.\) In the algorithm, End-while is highlighted, and then While \((r \gt 0)\) is highlighted. Step 7: In the next iteration, \(p\) is multiplied by \(s\) and has a new value of \(7 \cdot 7^4 \cdot 7^8\). \(s\) is squared and becomes \(7^{16}\). \(r\) becomes \((r \text{ div } 2\)) and has a new value of \(0.\) In the algorithm, If \((r ~\mbox{mod}~ 2 = 1)\) is highlighted next. An arrow labeled "last bit \(= r \text{ mod } 2\)" appears pointing to the last \(1\) in the binary rep of \(r\). \(p \colon= p \cdot s\) is now highlighted. The variable \(p\) is updated to \(1 \times 7 \times 7^4 \times 7^8\). \(s \colon= s \cdot s\) is now highlighted. Variable \(s\) is updated to \([(7^8)]^2 = 7^{8 \times 2} = 7^{16}\). \(r \colon= r \text{ div } 2\) is now highlighted. \(r\colon 1 \text{ div } 2 = 0\) is displayed, updating \(r\) to \(0\). Binary rep of \(r\) is updated to \(0\). In the algorithm, End-while is highlighted, and then While \((r \gt 0)\) is highlighted. Step 8: Since \( r = 0\), the loop is over. The algorithm returns \(p.\) The final value of \(p\) is \(7 \cdot 7^4 \cdot 7^8 = 7^{13}\). An arrow labeled "\(r = 0\) and the loop ends" appears pointing to the \(0\) in the binary rep of \(r.\) In the algorithm, End-while is highlighted, and then Return (\(p\)) is highlighted. Text: \(p = 1 \times 7 \times 7^4 \times 7^8 = 7^{1 + 4 + 8} = 7^{13}\). Algorithm returns \(7^{13}\).

6.1 Mathematical induction

5.4 Fast exponentiation
6.1 Mathematical induction
Learn to:

    Use terminology associated with mathematical induction.
    Identify the components of an inductive proof.
    Understand the format used in inductive proofs.
    Write inductive proofs for theorems about summations and algebraic inequalities.

Suppose that one day a genie grants Amal three wishes. Amal makes two wishes and then for his third wish, Amal wishes for three more wishes the next day. Given the fact that Amal can always use his third wish to renew his wishes for the next day, one can prove that from that first day onward, Amal can have three wishes every day for the rest of his life. This sequence of implications is an example of the principle of mathematical induction ( induction for short).

Induction is a proof technique that is useful for proving statements about elements in a sequence. An inductive proof establishes that some statement parameterized by is true, for any positive integer . In the example with the three wishes, the sequence is the sequence of days after that first day of wishes. The fact being proven is that you are able to have three wishes on the day for
Figure 6.1.1: The two components of an inductive proof.

    The base case establishes that the theorem is true for the first value in the sequence.
        The genie grants Amal three wishes on day

    The inductive step establishes that if the theorem is true for , then the theorem also holds for .
        If Amal has three wishes on day , then he can get three wishes for day (by using the third wish to ask for three more wishes the next day).

Feedback?

The principle of mathematical induction states that if the base case (for ) is true and the inductive step is true, then the theorem holds for all positive integers.
Figure 6.1.2: Principle of mathematical induction.

Let be a statement parameterized by a positive integer . Then is true for all positive integers , if:

    is true (the base case).
    For all , implies (the inductive step).

Feedback?

The inductive step is a compact way to state that an infinite sequence of implications are true:

For all , implies [ implies ] and [ implies ] and [ implies ] and …

In the statement " implies " of the inductive step, the supposition that is true is called the inductive hypothesis. The animation below illustrates why the principle of induction works by showing how the two components of an inductive proof imply that is true for any positive integer :
participation activity
6.1.1: Why induction works.
Start
Static figure: An explanation of why induction works. Step 1: \(P(1)\) is true, and for all \(k\ge 1\), \(P(k)\) implies \(P(k+1)\). Setting \(k = 1\) means that \(P(1)\) implies \(P(2)\). Step 2: Therefore, P(2) is also true. Step 3: For \(k = 2\), \(P(2)\) implies \(P(3)\). Therefore, \(P(3)\) is also true. For \(k = 3\), \(P(3)\) implies \(P(4)\). Therefore, \(P(4)\) is also true. Step 4: Since P(k) implies P(k + 1) for all k ≥ 1,the process can be continued up to any \(n ≥ 1\) Step 5: Therefore, for all \(n\ge1 \), \(P(n)\) is true.

6.2.3: The inductive step in the proof of the closed form for the sum of an arithmetic sequence.
Start
Static figure: Inductive hypothesis: \(\sum_{j = 0}^{k-1} (a + jd) = ka + \frac{d(k-1)k}{2}\) Prove: \(\sum_{j = 0}^{k} (a + jd) = a(k+1) + \frac{dk(k + 1)}{2}\) Step 1: Start with the left hand side of the fact to be proven and separate the last term from the summation: \(\sum_{j=0}^{k}(a + jd) = (a + kd) + \sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(a + jd)\). Step 2: Apply the inductive hypothesis by replacing \(\sum_{j=0}^{k}(a + jd)\) with \(ka + \frac {d(k-1)k}{2}\) to get \((a + kd) + ka + \frac{d(k-1)k}{2}\). Step 3: Rearrange the expression using algebra to get \(a + ka + \frac{2kd + d(k-1)k}{2}\). Step 4: Pull out common factors from terms to get \(a(k+1) + \frac{d(2k + k^2 - k)}{2}\) Step 5: Simplifying further gives \(a(k+1) + \frac{dk(k+1)}{2}\), which is equal to the initial expression \(\sum_{j=0}^{k}(a+jd)\).

participation activity
6.2.4: An inductive proof of the closed form for the sum of a geometric sequence.
Start
Static figure: For \(r \ne 1\), prove by induction: \(\sum_{j=0}^{n - 1} ar^j = \frac{a(r^n - 1)}{r-1}\). Step 1: The base case proves the theorem for \(n = 1\). \(\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{0} ar^j = ar^0 = a\). Also, \( \dfrac{a(r^1 - 1)}{r-1} = a\). The two expressions are equal. Step 2: The inductive step assumes that \(\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k-1} ar^j = \frac{a(r^k-1)}{r-1} \). Step 3: The inductive step proves that \(\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k} ar^j = \dfrac{a(r^{k + 1}-1)}{r-1} \). Step 4: The inductive step starts with the left side of the equality to be proven and pulls out the last term from the summation: \(\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k} ar^j = ar^k + \sum_{j=0}^{k - 1} ar^j \). Step 5: The inductive hypothesis is applied by replacing \(\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k - 1} ar^j \) with \(\dfrac{a(r^k -1)}{r-1} \) to get \( ar^k + \dfrac{a(r^k - 1)}{r-1} \). Step 6: The fractions are combined and simplified to get \( \dfrac{a[r^{k + 1} - r^k + r^k -1]}{r-1}\).
\(ar^k + \dfrac{a(r^k - 1)}{r-1}\) 	
\(= \frac{ar^k(r-1)}{(r-1)} + \frac{a(r^k - 1)}{r-1}\) 	The first fraction is multiplied by \(\frac{r-1}{r-1}\).
\(= \frac{a[r^k(r-1) + (r^k - 1)}{r-1}\) 	The two fractions are combined and an a is factored out of the numerator.
\(= \frac{a[r^{k+1} - r^k + r^k - 1]}{r-1}\) 	The \(r^k\) is distributed over \((r-1)\).
Step 7: The two middle terms in the numerator cancel, resulting in \(\dfrac{a(r^{k + 1} - 1)}{r-1}\), which is equal to the initial expression \(\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^k ar^j \).
Captions

An inductive proof for set operations

Induction is useful for proving theorems about other mathematical objects, beyond sequences and sums. The animation below uses induction to prove a generalized version of De Morgan's law for sets. The theorem assumes that De Morgan's law is true for two sets and shows that De Morgan's law can be extended to an arbitrary number of sets.
participation activity
6.2.6: Inductive proof of the generalized De Morgan's law for sets.
Start
Static figure: Inductive proof of generalized De Morgan's law for sets: \(\overline{A_1 \cap A_2 \cap \ldots \cap A_n} = \overline{A_1} \cup \overline{A_2} \cup \ldots \cup \overline{A_n}\) Step 1: Inductive proof of a Generalized De Morgan's law for sets. The base case for n = 2 is just De Morgan's law: \(\overline{A_1 \cap A_2} = \overline{A_1} \cup \overline{A_2} \). Step 2: The inductive step assumes the theorem for k: \( \overline{A_1 \cap A_2 \cap \ldots \cap A_k} = \overline{A_1} \cup \overline{A_2} \cup \ldots \cup \overline{A_k}\) and proves the theorem for k+1: \( \overline{A_1 \cap A_2 \cap \ldots \cap A_k \cap A_{k+1}} = \overline{A_1} \cup \overline{A_2} \cup \ldots \cup \overline{A_k} \cup \overline{A_{k+1}}\) Step 3: B is defined to be \( A_1 \cap A_2 \cap \ldots \cap A_k\). Therefore, \( \overline{A_1 \cap A_2 \cap \ldots \cap A_k \cap A_{k+1}} = \overline{B \cap A_{k+1}}\). Step 4: \( \overline{B \cap A_{k+1}} = \overline{B} \cup \overline{A_{k+1}}\) by De Morgan's law. Step 5: \(\overline{B} \cup \overline{A_{k+1}} = \overline{A_1 \cap A_2 \cap \ldots \cap A_k} \cup \overline{A_{k+1}}\) by the definition of B. Step 6: The result of applying the inductive hypothesis to the last expression is \( \overline{A_1} \cup \overline{A_2} \cup \ldots \cup \overline{A_k}\), which is equal to the first expression \( \overline{A_1 \cap A_2 \cap \ldots \cap A_k} \).

6.3 Strong induction and well-ordering

    6.3: Strong induction and well-ordering

6.2 More inductive proofs
6.3 Strong induction and well-ordering
Learn to:

    Understand the general principle of strong induction.
    Determine the number of base cases needed in a proof that uses strong induction.
    Use the principle of strong induction in mathematical proofs.
    Understand the well-ordering principle and its connection to mathematical induction.
    Use the well-ordering principle in mathematical proofs.

The Fibonacci sequence is defined by initial values and the recurrence relation for :

Suppose one wants to prove an upper bound on the Fibonacci numbers, such as for all In the standard form of induction, the inductive step would use the fact that to argue that However, since is defined in terms of and , the proof requires a bound on both and in order to upper bound . The inductive hypothesis for standard induction only provides the bound on .

For the upper bound on terms of the Fibonacci sequence, a stronger version of induction is required. The principle of strong induction assumes that the fact to be proven holds for all values less than or equal to and proves that the fact holds for By contrast the standard form of induction only assumes that the fact holds for in proving that it holds for Define to be the assertion that where is defined by Fibonacci's initial values and recurrence relation. The proof that is true for all nonnegative integers has two components:

    Base case: and are true.
    Inductive step: For every , implies .

In strong induction the inductive hypothesis is In regular (or weak) induction, the inductive hypothesis is just . Sometimes the inductive hypothesis is expressed using a universal quantifier. The following two ways to express the inductive hypothesis are equivalent:

is true.   ↔   For every in the range through , is true.
Figure 6.3.1: Expanded expression of the inductive step for weak and strong induction.
Comparison between the inductive step for weak and strong induction. Inductive step for weak induction: For all k ≥ 1, S(k) and S(k+1). When k=1: S(1) implies S(2). When k=2: S(2) implies S(3). When k=3: S(3) implies S(4). The pattern is shown to continue. Inductive step for strong induction: For all k ≥ 1, (S(0) and S(1) … and; S(k)) implies S(k+1). When k=1: (S(0) and S(1)) implies S(2). When k=2: (S(0) and S(1) and S(2)) implies S(3). When k=3: (S(0) and S(1) and S(2) and S(3)) implies S(4). The pattern is shown to continue.
Feedback?
participation activity
6.3.1: Using strong induction.

Suppose that the inductive step of a strong induction proof shows that for , if is true, then is true.
1)

2)
If one assumes that the inductive hypothesis is true, can one conclude that is true?
3)
If one assumes that the inductive hypothesis is true, can one conclude that is true?
Feedback?

There is no simple rule to determine when strong induction is required instead of the standard form of induction. The best method is to try proving the inductive step using only the preceding value (that is, try and prove the statement for using only the statement for ). If that fails, then try a stronger inductive hypothesis. The inductive proof showing a bound on the Fibonacci numbers given below would fail with standard induction because bounds on and are required to prove the bound for .

    :

Since , then . Therefore both and fall in the range from through , and by the inductive hypothesis, and .

Therefore, . ■

Generalized strong induction: multiple base cases

The predicate may be true only for greater than or equal to a particular value denoted below by the constant . Also, the base case may require proving the assertion directly for more than one value. The base case for a proof by strong induction establishes that holds for through , where and are constants. The inductive step in a proof by strong induction assumes that is true for all values of in the range from through some integer and then proves that theorem holds for .
Figure 6.3.2: Principle of strong mathematical induction.

Let be a statement parameterized by , a positive integer. Let be integers. Then is true for all if the following two conditions hold:

    are true (the base case).
    For all , if is true, then is also true (the inductive step).

Feedback?
Figure 6.3.3: Expanded expression of the inductive step with an arbitrary range for the base case.

The inductive step is shown here in expanded form. The base case must establish that the inductive hypothesis is true for the smallest value for k.
Base case: (S(a) and S(a + 1) and … and S(b)) Inductive step: or all k greater than or equal to b, (S(a) and S(a + 1) … and S(k)) implies S(k + 1). k = b: (S(a) and S(a + 1) and … and S(b)) implies S(b + 1). k = b + 1: (S(a) and S(a + 1) and … and S(b) and S(b+1)) implies S(b + 2). k = b + 2: (S(a) and S(a + 1) and … and S(b) and S(b+1) and S(b + 2)) implies S(b + 3). The pattern is shown to continue.

Strong induction example: buying cans of juice in -packs or -packs.

Cans of juice come in packs of three or four. A shopper wants to buy cans of juice by purchasing a combination of -packs or -packs. For which values of is this possible? Let be the assertion that cans of juice can be bought by purchasing only -packs or -packs. is true because the shopper can purchase one -pack and one -pack. is not true because -packs and -packs cannot be combined in any way to get five cans of juice. Strong induction will be used to prove that for any is true. The proof is outlined below:

    Base case: Prove , , and directly.
    Inductive step: For , assume that that is true for any in the range through , and prove that is true.
        Argue that is true by showing that falls in the range through , covered in the inductive hypothesis. The argument uses the fact that and therefore
        Since is true, cans of juice can be bought by combining -packs and -packs. By adding one -pack to the cans of juice, there will be cans of juice. Therefore is true.

participation activity
6.3.3: Inductive proof for buying cans of juice.
Start
Static figure: Proof that it's possible to buy \(n\) cans of juice by purchasing a combination of 3-packs or 4-packs when \(n \ge 6\). Step 1: The base case starts with \(n = 6\). Purchasing two 3-packs of juice results in 6 cans. Therefore, \(P(6)\) is true. Step 2: \(n = 7\) is next. Purchasing one 3-pack and one 4-pack results in 7 cans. Therefore, \(P(7)\) is true. Step 3: \(n = 8\) is next. Purchasing two 4-packs results in 8 cans. Therefore, \(P(8)\) is true. The base cases P(6), P(7), and P(8) have been proven directly. Step 4: The inductive hypothesis is that for \(k \ge 8\), P(j) is true for any \(j\) in the range \(6\) through \(k\). Step 5: The inductive step proves \(P(k+1)\). Start with \(k-2\) cans of juice. Step 6: Since \(k \ge 8\), then \(k−2 \ge 6\). Therefore, \(k-2\) is in the range \(6\) through \(k\) and by the inductive hypothesis, \(k-2\) cans of juice can be purchased. Step 7: When an additional 3-pack is purchased, the number of cans is \((k-2) + 3 = k+1\). Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
Captions
Feedback?
How many base cases?

Why are three base cases used in the example with the cans of -packs and -packs of juice? Determining how many base cases are required in a strong induction proof may require some trial and error, but some basic principles can be applied. In proving that is true, it is necessary to assume that is true in order to add one -pack to get cans of juice. If the base cases had only included and , then it would not have been possible to prove the inductive step for . In proving , it is necessary to assume but is not included in the base cases. The inductive step could have added a -pack at the end instead of a -pack, but using a -pack would have required consecutive bases cases ( through ) instead of consecutive base cases.

The principle of induction is closely related to another principle regarding the integers called the well-ordering principle:
Figure 6.3.4: The well-ordering principle.

The well-ordering principle says that any nonempty subset of the nonnegative integers has a smallest element.
Feedback?

The principle of mathematical induction, the principle of strong induction, and the well-ordering principle are all equivalent. In other words, using any one of the principles as an assumption, the other two principles can be proven. Below is a proof showing that if the well-ordering principle is true, then the principle of mathematical induction is true. The principle of induction is stated again below for review:
Figure 6.3.5: The principle of mathematical induction.

Let be a predicate that is parameterized by nonnegative integers . If the following two conditions hold:

    Base case: is true
    Inductive step: For all , implies

then for all , is true.

Feedback?
Proof 6.3.3: Well-ordering implies the principle of mathematical induction.
Proof.

We will show that the well-ordering principle implies the contrapositive of the principle of mathematical induction. In particular, we will show that if the well-ordering principle is true and the conclusion of the principle of mathematical induction is false, then at least one of the two hypotheses for induction must fail.

Suppose that the well-ordering principle is true. Suppose also that the conclusion of the principle of mathematical induction is false. Then is false for some Consider the set of integers such that and is false. By the well-ordering principle, there must be a smallest element in that set. because is not a member of the set. (One of the criteria for an integer to be included in the set is that .) If then is not true, and the base case fails. If then is true, but is false. Letting and is true, but is not true. Therefore, the inductive step fails for some Thus, if the statement is false for some then one of the two conditions for the principle of mathematical induction must be false.  ■
Feedback?

The well-ordering principle can be used to prove some facts directly. Typically, a proof that uses the well-ordering principle is a proof by contradiction. There is a statement , parameterized by an integer and the assertion to be proven is that is true for all for some constant . Define a set that consists of all integers that violate the assertion to be proven. The set contains all integers such that and is false. The well-ordering principle says that if the set is nonempty then it must have a smallest element. The details of the proof depend on the nature of the statement , but typically a contradiction arises by examining the smallest element in the set. Often the contradiction involves showing that there must be a yet a smaller value for which is false. If the set of all integers such that is false cannot have a smallest element, the set must be empty, and therefore must be true for all The well-ordering principle is illustrated in proving the following theorem:
Theorem 6.3.2: The Division Algorithm.
Let be an integer and a positive integer. There are unique integers and such that and

6.3 Strong induction and well-ordering
6.4 Recursive definitions
Learn to:

    Compute the value of a recursively defined function for a specific input.
    Apply recursive definitions for various sets, including sets of strings, binary trees, and integers.
    Apply a recursive definition of a set to show which items are in the set.
    Apply a recursive definition for a set to prove properties of the elements of the set.
    Create recursive definitions for various sets.

The factorial function for can be defined as:

A disadvantage of the definition given above is that the reader must infer what goes between the and the terms. Also, the value of is not clear from the definition. A more precise definition is such that:

The second definition is a recursive definition. In a recursive definition of a function, the value of the function is defined in terms of the output value of the function on smaller input values. One can use the recursive definition for to determine the value of the factorial function on a particular value for by starting at , multiplying by to get the value of then multiplying by to get , and so on, until the desired has been reached. The process is called recursion. Recursion is the process of computing the value of a function using the result of the function on smaller input values.
Figure 6.4.1: Using the recursive definition of to compute .
Recursive steps of the factorial function above to show that 4! = 24. 0! is 1. This result multiplied by 1 yields 1 times 0! = 1! or 1. This result multiplied by 2 yields 2 times 1! = 2! or 2. This result multiplied by 3 yields 3 times 2! = 3! or 6. This result multiplied by 4 yields 4 times 3! = 4! or 24.
Feedback?

Recursion can be used to define functions, sequences, and sets. For example, a recurrence relation (along with initial values) is a recursive definition of a sequence because the recurrence relation shows how to compute the value of a term as a function of terms with smaller indices (that is, terms that occur earlier in the sequence). A sequence is a special kind of function in which the domain is a consecutive set of integers. The notation for a sequence is slightly different since the input is specified with a subscript: A recurrence relation for sequence that is equivalent to the recursive definition for the factorial function is given below:

Recursively defined sets

Certain kinds of sets are most naturally specified with recursive definitions. A recursive definition of a set shows how to construct elements in the set by putting together smaller elements.
Definition 6.4.1: Components of a recursive definition of a set.

    A basis explicitly states that one or more specific elements are in the set.
    A recursive rule shows how to construct additional elements in the set from elements already known to be in the set. (There is often more than one recursive rule).
    An exclusion statement states that an element is in the set only if it is given in the basis or can be constructed by applying the recursive rules repeatedly to elements given in the basis.

Feedback?

Consider the task of specifying the correct syntax of a programming language, such as formally specifying that the parentheses within a statement must be properly nested. For simplicity, one can disregard the rest of the characters in the statement and focus on strings of left and right parentheses. For example, the string ( ( ) ( ) ) is properly nested, but the string ( ) ) is not. Formally specifying which strings are and are not properly nested is important because a compiler must systematically check that an expression is in the correct format and must issue an appropriate error statement if the expression is not in the correct format. Below is a recursive definition for the set of all strings of parentheses that are properly nested:
Figure 6.4.2: Recursive definition of the set of all properly nested parentheses.

    Basis: The sequence ( ) is properly nested.
    Recursive rules: If and are properly nested sequences of parentheses then:
        () is properly nested.
        is properly nested.
    Exclusion statement: a string is properly nested only if it is given in the basis or can be constructed by applying the recursive rules to strings in the basis.

Feedback?

Since the exclusion statement is essentially the same for every recursively defined set, it is often excluded (but implied) in a recursive definition of a set.

The basis states that the string ( ) is properly nested. Rule can be applied to ( ) to show that ( ( ) ) is also properly nested. Rule can be applied by taking two copies of ( ) and concatenating them to get the string ( ) ( ), which is also properly nested. Properly nested strings of length can be obtained by taking either one of the properly nested string of length and applying rule : ( ( ) ( ) ) and ( ( ( ) ) ). In addition, a properly nested string of length can be concatenated with a properly nested string of length to get properly nested strings of length : ( )( ( ) ), ( ( ) ) ( ), ( ) ( ) ( ).

In this definition, there is more than one way to apply the recursive rules to get the same string. For example, the string ( ) ( ) ( ) can be obtained by concatenating ( ) and ( ) ( ) in either order: ( ) ( ) ( ) or ( ) ( ) ( ). Some situations require having a set of recursive rules with only one way to construct each element in the set using the recursive rules.

Recursive definition for perfect binary trees

The next example is a recursive definition for a set of mathematical objects that are not strings. A tree has vertices (denoted by circles) and edges (denoted by line segments) that connect pairs of vertices. Not every collection of vertices and edges is a tree. A formal definition of trees and their properties is given elsewhere in this material. A recursive definition for a particular class of trees called perfect binary trees is given below. Each perfect binary tree has a designated vertex called the root.
Definition 6.4.2: Recursive definition for the set of perfect binary trees.

Basis: A single vertex with no edges is a perfect binary tree. The root is the only vertex in the tree.
A single circle labeled with v. The circle represents the root node with no edges, which is a perfect binary tree.

Recursive rule: If is a perfect binary tree, then a new perfect binary tree can be constructed by taking two copies of , adding a new vertex and adding edges between and the roots of each copy of . The new vertex is the root of T'.
Diagram of binary tree T'. Root node is v, which contains two edges that each point to a copy of T. The root node is a circle with a v inside it. The copies of T are symbolized by a triangle with a circle at the top of it.
Feedback?
participation activity
6.4.8: Using the recursive rule to construct perfect binary trees from smaller ones.
Start
Static figure: Examples of perfect binary trees. Step 1: The tree \(T\) consisting of a single node is a perfect binary tree. A copy of the single node tree moves down to the list of perfect binary trees. Step 2: To create a new tree, \(T\) is duplicated. The single node tree, \(T\), is duplicated. The copy moves to the right on the same level as the original. Step 3: A new vertex \(v\) is added, along with edges from \(v\) to the roots of the two copies of \(T\). The two copies of \(T\) move down and another vertex is added above the copies of \(T\). Lines from \(v\) to each t are drawn. Step 4: The result is a perfect binary tree with three vertices. Call the new tree \(T\). A copy of \(T\) moves down to the list of perfect binary trees. Step 5: \(T\) is duplicated and a new vertex \(v\) is added, along with edges from \(v\) to the roots of the two copies of \(T\), resulting in a perfect binary tree with 7 vertices. The three node tree, \(T\), is duplicated and a new vertex \(v\) is added above the copies. Edges are added from v to the roots of the two trees \(T\). The result is a perfect binary tree with \(7\) vertices. Call the new tree \(T\). A copy of \(T\) moves down to the list of perfect binary trees.

6.4.10: A recursive definition for full binary trees.
Here is a definition for a set of trees called full binary trees.

Basis: A single vertex with no edges is a full binary tree. The root is the only vertex in the tree.
A single circle labeled with v. The circle represents the root node with no edges, which is a full binary tree

Recursive rule: If and are full binary trees, then a new tree can be constructed by first placing to the left of , adding a new vertex at the top and then adding an edge between and the root of and an edge between and the root of . The new vertex is the root of .
\(T^\prime\) contains root vertex v. There is an edge on the left from v to T1 and an edge on the right from v to T2. T1 and T2 are different subtrees. The subtrees are represented by a triangle with a circle at the top. T's triangle is larger than T1's

The placement of the trees on the left and right makes a difference. For example, the two trees below are different full binary trees:
The tree on the left has a root with a left edge pointing to a vertex that points to two vertices below it and a right edge pointing to a vertex with no vertices below it. The tree on the right has a root with a left edge pointing to a vertex with no vertices below it and a right edge pointing to a vertex that points to two vertices below it.
(a)

Draw all possible full binary trees with three or fewer vertices.
(b)

Draw all possible full binary trees with five vertices.
(c)

Draw all possible full binary trees with seven vertices.
(d)

The function maps every full binary tree to a positive integer. is equal to the number of vertices in . Give a recursive definition for .
(e)

The function maps every full binary tree to a nonnegative integer. is called the height of tree and is defined to be the maximum number of edges reached in a path that starts at the vertex and always travels downward in the tree.
